22. **The orientation ledger.** **The remembered-route list stands at TEN, the original eight intact, none removed, all ten marked by somebody who is not him, and it has not moved since the ninth of December.** The ninth crossing's failure to cost a route is still unexplained and **is not explained by anything in these ten chapters.** **Milo Rook crossed nothing in Chapters 71–80, at his own decision, in public, in his own book — I did not cross and I am not going to, and the yard is not asking me to — and Iona Sen's tally has nine lines on it and the count is still nine.** **Ownership of the tally, fixed here so that a later batch does not move it by accident: the paper is Hettie Brann's — a clinic counter form in her own hand, on the back of a form, as it has been since Volume 01 Chapter 56 — and it is Dr Sen's in her apron pocket, where it has been since she wrote the ninth line on it on the twenty-ninth of December and put it away before anybody asked her for it, and Chapter 80 says both halves in the same sentence. It is not handed to Hettie Brann in this batch, and the return-route holder and the tally holder are two different women in two different rooms and may not be conflated.** It is the first batch in the volume in which he does not cross, and that is a decision and not an absence.
23. **The annual maintenance return, and the box about persons, and the form the district cannot get.** **A printed single-side form, in use since about 1968, about four hundred of them in the authority, one per section per January.** Its boxes: *lengths held by this section; total length, chainage; condition summary; **persons observed on the length during the year**; action taken by the officer; officer; date; counter-signature.* **The fifth box is a good box and a man in a cutting is the reason it exists and it is not sinister**, and it is filled **nil**, every year, by a works clerk of forty-six, for the reason given out loud in a yard: **if he writes persons in there then next January there is a return in a file that says persons were observed on the quay service lengths, and the only name available to be asked is the foreman's, and there is no column on that form for the answer.** **A nil on a length is not a nil on a street, because there is nobody on a length to be observed and there is a gang on it, and those are two different facts and they are in the same box.** **And the district's counter-argument — write the crew in it — does not survive being written out loud, because the crew was not observed; the crew was the observer.** The box will be filled in again in January and in every January after this one and the only true answer to it is nil, and that is the batch's not-curable cost.
24. **The sixth line, and the only true sentence about the thirteenth of October in a document of the authority.** **Fifty-four words**, a clerk's own hand, **on the return of the fourteenth of January**, signed *C. SALE, clerk, 14.i.*: *Length PW/Q/4 (back length) added to this schedule at 4th edn., 22 Nov, per service amendment 12/44 (eff. 13 Oct), under standing instruction 11/D/6. Margin note in my hand. 3rd edn. (11 Sept) did not contain the length. **The amendment's lines for requested by and instructing officer are blank and I cannot supply either.*** **The clerk wrote it on the Wednesday morning at about ten past ten and the foreman signed the return on the same Wednesday morning at about eleven, with the sixth line not read out to him, because a foreman reads a return the way a foreman reads a return, which is the first, second, third and fourth lines. There is no copy. The district has no standing to ask for one. Milo Rook does not know and will not find out in this volume.** *That is where a true sentence goes. Everywhere else it is a speech.*
25. **Mara Quill's nine-minute letter, and what she said afterwards and did not say.** Friday the sixteenth of January, to the company secretary of the tram authority, nine minutes, in her own hand and her own name, with a two-year note on her conduct: she asks that the annual maintenance return of the quay section be produced to the reporting officer at the adjourned hearing in original and not as an extract, and states **I have no right to the document I ask for and I do not assert one.** **She says, in a corridor, that the rest of it — the reason she cannot write the letter properly — is about a piece of paper in a drawer in her own flat, that it has nothing to do with an annual return, that she has not said it and is not going to, and that she has written down in advance that she was not going to and will read it back to herself on the day.** The custody register page is still in a drawer with a coat in it and has now been demanded once and refused once and **is not confessed to.**
26. **The return route, version two, and its holder, and it is neither of them.** On **Sunday the eighteenth of January** — the seventeenth is a Saturday in this batch's own chain — a foreman of fifty-four wrote seven lines in grease pencil in the corner of the card, unasked, for nobody: *The gate is shut Mondays and Thursdays 07.30 to about 08.30, for the walk, and the key is on a nail inside the hut and eleven men need it. The rest of the week it is on the latch until four and after that it is a padlock and the padlock is the company's. Do not go through that compound in a car. There is nowhere to turn. **This is not a route and I am not redrawing anybody's route.** R.P., 18.i.* The card goes to **version two** in Milo Rook's hand, because he is the only one of the three or four people who writes dates. **For the first time the four copies differ: three say version one and one says version two.** **Four copies is not a document; three copies of a thing and one other copy of a thing is a thing two people disagree about.** From Monday the nineteenth the holder is **Hettie Brann, sixty-one, nineteen years on the water clinic counter**, who was asked for a route in December and said no and has not changed her mind, and who takes the envelope on one condition and refuses to be thanked. Her rule, in her own hand: **first working day of the month — the four copies are taken out of the four places and looked at, the version and the date are compared and nothing else is read; if one of them is not the same, ask the person whose hand it is and nobody else; the holder is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be.** The first working day of the month was the first of January and **there was no holder then and nobody knows whether the four copies were the same**; the next ones are the second of February, the second of March and the first of April. **And her condition on her own illness: then the round does it, and the round is a woman with a satchel who has said in this yard that she is not going to be the person anybody leans on, and she is going to be anyway, and I would like that written down as the second cost of this card and not as a favour.**
27. **The run number, and the plant, and it is not looked up.** The working manifest has six columns, one of which is *run no*, and every booked working has a run number against it, and **a run number is not a destination.** Hester Coombe can look it up in four minutes in a book on a shelf in her office and has not, and says so, and asks him on Wednesday the twenty-first and says yes, and a card goes face up under a brick that afternoon reading: *The run number on the carbon of the 07:52, 16 Dec. To be looked up in the working timetable. Not before Friday. — H.C.* **It is still under the brick on the Thursday morning, nobody in this district chases it, and a man of thirty-one walked past it about four times a day and read it and did not pick it up, and the reason is on the page in eleven words — *a card under a brick is a thing, not a job* — and it is the last rule this district has made and it is not a rule. **This chapter does not narrate past the twenty-first of January and the run number is not looked up in this batch.** The volume's final image — a recovered timetable containing a destination that does not exist with the passengers' names real in it — is not written in this batch and is not a thing this batch was permitted to write.**
28. **Mireward, and what a query returns, and the absence of an office.** A district returns query on a printed form does not go to a district office, **because there is no district office and there never has been.** **The query was filled in at about half past two on the Friday the ninth in the route room and went into the internal post the same afternoon, and what came back on the Friday the sixteenth was a printed card, seven days later, and the seven days are not the finding** — the finding is that a query asks a district office a question about a quarter and there is no district office and there is not a person to be rude. From a man of about fifty in the drainage section with nineteen years in the room: **Mireward is on this city's returns because a return list is printed from a list of districts and that list is a hundred and forty years old and it has never been printed from a list of offices. A man of fifty-eight goes out on the first dry week with a rod and a chain and has been going since 1974 and writes four lines on a pad, and the pad goes in a drawer in this office from this year back to about 1969, and nobody has ever asked him for one of those pads. A man who goes out in a summer and writes four lines about a drain is not an office and does not become one, and in forty-one years nobody has ever written the word office next to that name on a piece of paper.** What comes back instead is a printed card out of a machine: *District: **MIREWARD.** Return required: quarterly ground and seam return. Last received: —. Reason outstanding: —. **Officer responsible: [blank].*** **Mireward may be named, queried and sent about in these ten chapters and is not explained, is not entered as a case, and nobody goes there.**
29. **The forecast, acted on, and the office with nobody behind it.** Four pages from the office of the First Surveyor, for the season from the second of January to the fourteenth of April, **forecasting eleven weather-driven seam events, four of them in the river core, one in the Outer Reach, the rest unallocated and allocated on the day, with the first expected in the last week of January, which is the week of Monday the twenty-sixth.** **Three people act on it and two of them are right: Dr Iona Sen opens the water clinic every day of that week from eight until six, and Rue takes the round out at eight and in at seven for seven days instead of five, unpaid, and says the price out loud.** The forecast itself **cannot be entered in any register in this city** — no series number, no distribution list, no return, no address, and it arrived in the hand of a man of about fifty who said *it is for the route room*, said the name of the office, and left. **The office of the First Surveyor is named a third time and no person is produced behind it, and Milo Rook's standing reason for not asking is on the page: I would be asking whether there is a man behind it, and if there is not then the forecast is a machine, and if there is then it is a man, and I do not want either answer this month and both of them are going to arrive anyway.** Dilley Farr's answer, which is the other half of a December sentence: **nothing is a very good answer and it is very hard to argue with, and the moment you put nothing in a column it stops being nothing and becomes a hole with a heading on it, and a heading is a thing somebody can look up.**
30. **The round, and the gate, and the seven days, and the two prices that are said out loud.** The round is seven stops in eleven streets, out at eight and in at four, unpaid, and **Pell Harrow being up the south for twenty-two days made it one man short, which does not mean somebody else does more, it means it takes longer — about twenty-two minutes longer on the Thursday the eighth.** From the Monday the fifth the round **goes through the quay section gate on Mondays and Thursdays only**, four minutes longer each way, at the foreman's condition that there is a lock on the hut and the key is on a nail and eleven men need it, **not on a schedule, not paid for, and not asked for.** Rue's reason, in a yard: *a tram authority's amendment committee cannot hear anything about a stop on a round, and I am not going to stand in a canteen and ask a man to put my round in a book so that it looks like the same fight. It is a gate that is open twice a week.*
31. **Pell Harrow, and the blank card.** He went up the south on **Sunday the twenty-eighth of December** and came back on **Monday the nineteenth of January — twenty-two days — on his own money**, and did not telephone, and was on the round at eight on the Monday, and **said nothing about where he had been, and nobody asked.** His card is **blank** — a courier's card about two inches by three with a line ruled across the middle and a date in his own hand at the bottom and nothing on either side — and it went face up under the brick on the trestle table. **Tern is not found, the list in the tin is not a finding, Ostrow confirms nothing, and no chapter in this block rescues anybody.** Rue: *He is back and he is on the round and he is not going to say anything about it and nobody is going to ask him, and I would like that written down by somebody who is not me, and I know who is going to read this in a year and it is not a person in this yard.*
32. **The works jacket, third sighting, and the standing rule.** A man of about fifty with a badge that says a number and not a name was on the quay on **Monday the fifth of January at about ten past eight, forty yards off the north end, in a doorway out of the rain, notebook out, wrote twice, and was not there on the Thursday the eighth.** The card went face up under a brick. **Nobody followed him on either day, and Rue's rule, said in a yard: he is not ours and we are not following him and I have said that twice in three months and I would rather be wrong on a Tuesday than right on a Sunday. He is unidentified and unresolved and he must not become a face, a faction, or an antagonist, and this batch adds no name and no copy and no copy seen.**
33. **Volume 01's people carry Volume 01's prices into this batch and none of them is refunded, and this is stated once, in a Flat inventory, in Milo Rook's own Field Book on the twenty-first of January, and nothing in Chapters 71–80 moves any of it.** **A woman of sixty-eight on nothing** — the fourth reference to her in Volume 02 across four chapters, always as *a woman of sixty-eight*, never by name, and **this batch is adding one and says so here; nobody went up three flights of stairs about her and she is not named, thanked, argued back, put on anything, or solved.** Two children lost in October and still lost, and no third. A hundred and forty feet of named frontage with a settlement plate and a date. A gated, silted, re-aligned culvert that is not the culvert that was there before the order. **A man asleep on a third floor of a building with no number, not written down and not woken, in a clause of his own.** A man of forty-one behind a boatyard on no list at all. A mechanic of sixty-one on a drainage record forever who will not swear. A conductor of thirty in a printed rule book whose hearing is still not set. A woman of fifty-four with a plot number and no bill in six years. A foreman of thirty-one years who will be asked in about four months what he saw and who has not written one word about a person on a form. Two and four a head a week, in advance, with no term, in a building with no number. **And the two money totals are carried forward unchanged and the multiplication is still never shown, because the two totals are inconsistent at the shilling and that is inherited from Chapter 38 and is not repaired.**
34. **The clue chain is carried and not advanced, and is not touched in this block at all.** The copied tick in a fourth column in six houses in four streets, the copied turn sheet with a contractor's head on it, the carbon relocation schedule with its one mark, the four by six gutter copying, the pair of marks and the pair of marks, the three references, and the routing slip with *for index* on it **appear in no chapter of Chapters 71–80 as a document and are joined to nothing.** **The second tin is not a fourth instance of anything and is not joined to anything. Two documents still carry a pair of marks and not three; the twenty-first's Registry certification is drawn where the two confirmed marks are pressed, a difference in method and not in age; Kest has not matched the strings; and Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–80.** A fold is not a chronology and no document's age in this batch is established by a mark on it. **No new mark, no new document, no new hand, and no new instance of the standing instruction beyond the two already on the page.**
35. **No new grammar, faction, antagonist, final enemy, or final threat.** The grammars in use in these ten chapters are **Keepsake** (a market of eleven habits) and the block does not name a dominant grammar for anything new. **A length remains a third kind of place and is not a remnant, is not a failure state, and is not a form of any of the seven grammars.** **Sel Iri does not appear. Halden Vey does not appear and is not named. Orrin Sable does not appear. Tarin Kest does not appear. Amos Trench, Bettina Sallow and Thread Hall do not appear in these ten chapters and the withdrawal file is not touched.** The antagonists of this block are **a rule of a committee, a list day, a form in a filing cabinet, and a line under the words *officer responsible*, and every one of them is correct.**
36. **The relationship, milestone 2, and it is a working instrument and it is not warm.** Three hands, four copies, a version number, **a version two and one copy that differs, a holder who is neither of the two people who made it, and a rule at the foot that names the holder's exclusions.** **Nothing romantic has happened and nothing is going to. No kiss, no declaration, no jealousy scene, no rescue. The Lark Street grievance is not named, not resolved, not softened and not referred to in these ten chapters. Her knowledge of the token and the custody column is demanded once, refused once, and still unused; he does not know she refused anything, and he watches her look at a table for about four seconds and writes down something else.** The trust between them in this batch is procedural and practical and consists of two people who have both been shown a document they should not have seen, **and of one of them saying out loud, in a room, that the other has not been asked and is not going to be.**


## What Chapters 81–90 fixed on the page, in the same form as CR-013

Each item below is now prose rather than plan, and each is load-bearing in a way a later batch could break without intending to. **None of them changes a premise, a world rule, a power rule, the antagonist ladder, or the planned ending. Every figure here is quotable from a chapter in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0081.md` … `chapter-0090.md`, and this list was written from the manuscript, with the standing caveat from the section above: a Field Book day-count is quotable only from the chapter that owns the day, and every one of the ten in this batch is written in the chapter that owns its day.** Chapters 81–90 are the **third block of Volume 02** — the run-up to the season — and they inherit the Chapters 61–70 and 71–80 lists above whole.

1. **The batch's shape and its boundary.** Ten chapters, **Monday the twenty-sixth of January to Friday the twentieth of February**, twenty-six days. **It is the run-up to the season and not its resolution, it does not resolve the volume, and it does not write the volume's final image.** The volume's immediate promise was answered in Batch 0001 and is not re-opened; the hearing was held and adjourned in Batch 0002 and is not re-opened. **The first week of February is the hinge, because Monday the second of February is the first working day of the month and therefore the first day the return-route holder compares four copies of a card.**

2. **The weekday chain, extended and not re-derived.** From Batch 0002, which left the chain at 21st Wed, 22nd Thu: **23rd Fri, 24th Sat, 25th Sun, 26th Mon, 27th Tue, 28th Wed, 29th Thu, 30th Fri, 31st Sat, 1st February Sun, 2nd Mon, 3rd Tue, 4th Wed, 5th Thu, 6th Fri, 7th Sat, 8th Sun, 9th Mon, 10th Tue, 11th Wed, 12th Thu, 13th Fri, 14th Sat, 15th Sun, 16th Mon, 17th Tue, 18th Wed, 19th Thu, 20th Fri**, then the fixed points: **1st March Sun, first working day Monday the 2nd, 1st April Wed, 14th April Tue.** A later batch may not re-derive a weekday and may not move the second of February, the second of March, the first of April or the fourteenth of April. **The chapter dates, all on the page: 26 Jan Mon, 29 Jan Thu, 30 Jan Fri, 31 Jan Sat, 1 Feb Sun, 2 Feb Mon (twice), 5 Feb Thu, 12 Feb Thu, 17 Feb Tue, 18 Feb Wed, 19 Feb Thu, 20 Feb Fri.**

3. **The gang's week, and the four sheets, and the tin's arithmetic, which is the count most likely to be got wrong.** The gang is on the back length **Mondays and Thursdays at 07.30**, the weekly sheet covers Monday to Sunday, it goes into the rack on the Friday, the duplicate into a tin, and the duplicate into the second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room of the Civic Atlas Office under the repair schedule. **In this block the walks are the 26th, the 29th, the 2nd, the 5th, the 9th and the 12th of January and February, and the sheets go into the rack on the 30th of January, the 6th, the 13th and the 20th of February.** **The second tin held four weeks of gang carbon plus the one page that is not a gang sheet on Friday the sixteenth of January; it holds SEVEN weeks plus that page on Friday the sixth of February, and NINE weeks plus that page on Friday the twentieth of February.** **The reason column is empty on every walk in these ten chapters, and the condition column carries three words — *Cover reset. Rocking.* — on the walks of the 2nd, the 5th, the 9th and the 12th of February, and four words — *Cover up. Ballast washed. Hole.* — on the 29th of January.** All of these are quotable from Chapters 81, 85 and 88.

4. **The first weather-driven seam event, and it is a drain cover, and it happened at about twenty past two in the morning on Thursday the twenty-ninth of January and nobody was awake for it.** Rain off the south-west for about forty minutes. The gang was on the length at half past seven the same morning, eleven men, nineteen degrees below freezing, and the trolley wheels had a noise on them that morning. **The man of about twenty-six stopped in the down loop and went no nearer than about nine feet, which is the first time in two years he has not gone down to the cover, and he got a shovel instead and said nothing.** The cover — two marks on it, a cross and a bar, put there by a man called Ferrier in about 1971 — **was up out of its frame by about two inches with the ballast washed out from under about a foot of it and a clean hole about the size of a dinner plate at the north side of the frame, the iron rusted brown underneath the flange and clean on the top face.** The foreman wrote four words in the condition column and the reason column is empty. **His answer, in nine words, and the truest thing anybody said about that morning and not improved on since: *It filled. There was rain in the night… I did not come down here, because it is a drain and it is not a road and nobody was on a car.*** **Nobody was on a car. Nobody was hurt. Nothing collapsed.**

5. **The after-effects, and they are ordinary, and two inches is not a flood.** **Two inches standing in the low streets by about ten on the morning of Friday the thirtieth, in a band about forty feet wide along the Cardew Street side and across onto the Ferry Road side, against a district flood line of nine inches, and four inches in a month with nine-tenths of an inch of rain is on the page from the twelfth of October.** Nine inches of water inside the back of the cold store on a floor that slopes. **The boatyard boards at the low end under** — three new boards nailed in the second week of the previous year over a rotten channel crossing — **and the only way round is the wall**, in Doreen Sankey's words: *I could not get the boards under at the low end and I went round the wall, and the wall is the only way round and it is not a road.* **Nothing in this block re-opens anything about the flood of the twelfth of October or the culvert, and the culvert is not mentioned.**

6. **The clinic and the round, and the failure of both, in eleven words, and it is the batch's first finding.** A water clinic counter open **from eight until six for seven days of the week of Monday the twenty-sixth**, and a courier's round **out at eight and in at seven for the same seven days**, both decided by one woman each, both unpaid, and **neither of them able to say which day they were for, because the four pages say the first event is expected in the week of the twenty-sixth and that the remainder are allocated on the day, and there is nowhere on four pages to say a day.** **Dr Iona Sen wrote both facts in one line of the counter's own day book on the Friday morning with no mark between them, and said at the counter: *The clinic was shut four hours before it and I am writing both of those down together on purpose and I am not putting a line between them… a water clinic is not a thing that is open at two o'clock in the morning, and the day is the whole of what a person needs.*** **And she said it six times in a doorway on the Sunday and asked for it to be minuted and nobody minuted it and about four people noticed that nobody had.** A further finding, in the same book: a counter closed for its half hour between one o'clock and half past one is **the only half hour it is ever shut for**, and the card in the frame by the door was turned round on the Monday.

7. **The seven doors, all seven still there, and four more, and the count is eleven.** The seven of the first of January are unchanged. **The four that come out of this block, all in the same seven weeks: the SEAM EVENT ALLOCATION sheet out of a drainage-section machine; the printed DISTRICT RETURNS card out of the same machine; the four pages of the seasonal outlook on a table in Crown Heights, still not in a register since the twenty-eighth of December; and a form in the tram authority's internal file for which there is now a filing reference and a date of completion and no copy.** **And the honest outcome, unchanged and not improved on: a thing happened on the twenty-ninth of January and this city has no instrument for showing anybody that it happened, and a second thing happened on the nineteenth of February and this city has no row for it, and both of those are the same hole, and the hole is that the city's paperwork is built out of places.** Every one of the eleven is correct and not one of them is ever going to be anything else.

8. **The SEAM EVENT ALLOCATION sheet, in full, and it is a new object in this city and not a new institution.** A single side, printed, out of the same machine as the district returns cards, **about nine hundred of which come off it a month.** Issued with the seasonal outlook **under the office of the First Surveyor**. Its columns are **EVENTS FORECAST / EVENT / ALLOCATED TO / OFFICER RESPONSIBLE**, and it has **five rows: RIVER CORE 4, CROWN HEIGHTS, SOUTH SALT, OUTER REACH 1, MIREWARD 0.** **The *events forecast* column is not typed — it is the four pages' own figures transferred by hand with a pen by a man of about fifty, fifty-one next month, nineteen years in the room**, and a later batch may not make it a printed column. The line at the foot: *An allocation which cannot be returned is not an outstanding allocation; it is a sheet in a drawer.* **Its whole purpose, in nine words: *a sheet like that is for having something on the day to give somebody, so that a district is told, so that if a district says it was told, there is a piece of paper with its name on it. It has never once told anybody what happened. It has only ever been the thing that was given.*** And: **an allocation is a claim that somebody was told, and the gap between those two things in this city is about eleven months and a filing cabinet, and that gap is where this season is going to happen.**

9. **The first entry in the city, on any form, about the twenty-ninth of January, in five words and a time, and it is the only record of the first event.** In the river core row, in a man's own hand, on the Friday the thirtieth: > *29.i. 02.20. Cover up in loop, ballast washed. Back length. No persons. — G.S.R., 30.i.* **It says *no persons*, because the form has no other way of being finished, and *no persons* is the only honest answer available on that night and it is the answer the sheet wanted and not the answer anybody wanted.** **There is no copy of it, it is not on a return, and it is not on the gang's sheet, whose reason column was empty on that walk and is on every walk.**

10. **The correct refusal of Chapter 82, and its cost in seven days, and both are on the page.** Four of the five *officer responsible* boxes were filled, **three of the four surnames off a wall board in that room that has never once been wrong about a building, and none of the four confirmed this year.** The river core box reads **D. Farr, route room, Civic Atlas Office**, and the text says plainly that it is not even true. **The Mireward box was left empty and the reason, in about nine words: *If I put a name in the box for a district, then in about four years somebody asks that person what they were doing on the night of the twenty-ninth of January, and there is no column on this form for the answer.*** **The sheet went back on the Friday the thirtieth with a line missing off it, and on Friday the sixth of February, seven days later, the same machine printed a DISTRICT RETURNS card whose reason column carried a sentence where it had carried the machine's dash: *Reason outstanding: allocation sheet w/e XXVI, officer column uncompleted.* A sentence is a thing a person can be sent to look at, and the seven days are the machine's and not the finding.**

11. **Mireward, the three nils, and the fact that nobody has been.** **A typed 0 in the events-forecast row of the allocation sheet on the thirtieth of January; a dash and three blanks on the printed card of the sixteenth of January; a nil in a district stability column entered on the twenty-second of December by an office that signed no name, on the ground that there is no maintained seam, no recorded loss, no resident witness and no schedule.** **Three in nine weeks and the third is a man with a pen, and a later batch may not make it four.** The finding, in his own words: ***nothing about Mireward is a record of Mireward. A place is not stable because nothing has happened in it. It is entered as nil because there has been nobody to write down what is in it, and the two things look identical on a form and they are not the same thing, and the second one is worse.*** **A man of fifty-eight goes out on the first dry week of every summer with a rod and a chain and writes four lines about about nine feet of drain into a pad in a drawer in Crown Heights from this year back to about 1969 and nobody has ever asked him for one, and in forty-one years nobody has ever written the word office next to that name on a piece of paper. Nobody went there in this block, nobody is entered as a case, and it is not explained.**

12. **The failure, in Chapter 84, and it is his, and it is the seventh column for the third time in five weeks.** On the evening of Saturday the thirty-first, between about nine and eleven, at a kitchen table: a form headed **SEAM EVENTS — RIVER CORE — WEEKLY**, the gang's own six columns copied off a weekly sheet, and a seventh column headed **SCHEDULED**, which **is not a question and is a column of days.** He filled nine days of it in nine minutes **off a card about two inches by three in a frame at the water clinic, which says the counter is open from eight until six and that the round passes at about a quarter past eight and about a quarter to four, and which is there because a nurse needs to know when a woman with a satchel is going to walk past the door.** **A thing built for one purpose and the wrong shape for another, and the sentence for it: *a card with a time on it looked, from about nine o'clock, like a way of being sure.*** He read it out standing up in a yard of about nineteen on the evening of Sunday the first of February.

13. **What the failure cost, in four mouths, and the fourth is the worst thing he has done in five months.** **Rue**, who read it out after him word for word: *You have written my round in a column with a day against it, and I did not give you the days… you have got it off a card on a wall because there is a nurse in that room who needs to know when I walk past.* **Lena Marr**, in about forty seconds of arithmetic: *if she stops — and she will stop — then there is a piece of paper in a drawer in Crown Heights with a date on it that says the round went out at eight and in at seven for the week of the second of February. And that paper will not know whether it happened or not, and it will say it happened, and in about four years somebody in an office will be able to look at it and say that the round ran, and they will be right, because it is on the form.* **That is our tram.** **Dr Iona Sen**, one question from the back of the yard in a wind, **What happens on it the day it does not come** — and **his own answer, out loud, in a yard, in about eleven seconds, and it cannot be unsaid**: *then this district is a district that was expecting weather-driven seam events on the ninth of February, and there is a form, and the ninth of February is on the form, and on the tenth of February a man in an office can put his finger on the ninth of February and say: this district expected one on the ninth and did not have one… and Mireward is entered as nil on four pages on the stated ground that there is no maintained seam, no recorded loss, no resident witness and no schedule, and is marked entered as stable. And I have just written the column that makes this district the same kind of thing, and the only difference between this district and Mireward is that about nine people have been in this district and nobody has been in Mireward.* **He has made the case for a zero against his own district in about eleven seconds and a doctor has said so in a yard and nobody is going to forget it.**

14. **What became of the form, in four sentences, and none of them is good for him.** **He did not tear it up.** He put it face up in daylight on a trestle table, struck the seventh column, dated the strike, **wrote no reason** — because the first time he did that there was a woman's name under the line with no date and no reason, and that page is in a tin on a bottom shelf in a building nine hundred yards from that yard. He wrote the four objections on the back **in the words of the four people who had made them**, and **he did not consult Rue, and said so in front of nineteen people.** And **the sheet went back into the inside pocket of a coat with a button off and it stayed there**, on the stated ground: *there is nowhere in this city to put a bad idea of mine that is safer than me, and the tin is a drawer nine hundred yards away and the drawer is a building I work in, and a coat is a thing I take home.* **Dr Iona Sen's finding on the third of these, and it is the standing cost of the January page: *you have got better at hiding them. Getting better at hiding them is not the same as being right, and it is further from being right than the first one was.***

15. **The changed method, and it is not his, and it is the second side of the sheet of who does what and on which day.** Made on the morning of Monday the second of February in about eleven minutes, **headed in the fishmonger's hand and ruled in her hand**, and Milo Rook was not consulted, has said so in front of about eleven people twice, and had no advice to give. > *THE SAME SHEET, OTHER SIDE* / *A day nobody stood in. A thing a day did to a place. Not right, not wrong, not a person.* / **The rule at the foot, in her hand, two sentences: *Nothing on this side is anybody's fault and nothing on this side is a reason. A side with nothing on it is a day when nobody stood in the weather.*** **The first side of that sheet is not superseded and keeps its four rules, and the column headed *wrong* keeps its rule, and the reason they are two sides is stated on the page: a line on the first side is somebody doing a thing on a day, and a thing a person does can be right or wrong or changed, and the ground is not somebody and the day is not somebody.**

16. **The first initials on the *wrong* column, and the first line on the second side, and the difference between them, which is the whole of the method.** **On the first side, a fishmonger of about forty-five — and she is the first person ever to put initials on that column — after a man of about forty sweeping the Row badly asked her at ten to six on the Monday morning whether a piece of paper in a yard was right, and she read it and brought it up the Row at about ten past ten.** Her correction, in her words: *I do it at about ten past five and then I put my thumb in a crack in the wall for about half a minute, and that is what is written, and it is not the half of it… and on Friday morning, when there was water across the footway from the loop end, I did it from my own doorway, because I was not going to stand in it. And that is not the same thing. And this sheet has got a column for whether a thing is right and a column for whether a thing is wrong, and it has got nothing at all for the same thing done somewhere else.* **The first line on the second side is the man of about twenty-six's, in his own hand, and he put his initials against it, which is new, and he will still never initial the first side, and the reason is the whole of it: *the first side is about me, and you do not put your name on a line saying you have stopped doing a thing… this side is not about me, this side is about the day, and I was on that day because the cover is on my walk, and if somebody asks me in four years I will say I stood in it and I will not have to say why, and that is the difference between a person and a day and I have not got a word for the difference and the difference is my initials.***

17. **The cost of the second side, named out loud at the time and not hidden.** **It is a column that says how many days somebody stood in the weather, and a form that says how many times is a form somebody will ask about in a year.** Rue's own words: *it is the same box with different words on it and one of the words is how many times, and I have put it there anyway, and I have told you it is there, and I would rather it be a thing we all know about than a thing we find out in April.* **By Friday the twentieth of February it has four lines on it so far; by the fourteenth of April it is expected to have about nine blanks.**

18. **The recovery, and it is somebody else's work, and it is not a power and does not scale.** On the afternoon of **Monday the second of February, the first working day of the month**, Hettie Brann took the four copies out of the four places and compared the version and the date and nothing else. **Three say version one and one says version two, and the only one of the four people who has ever put a date on that card cannot say which is right**, in his own words: *a version number is a claim, and a version number with four copies in four buildings and nobody reading any of them is a rumour with a date on it.* At about ten past five, in a corridor, she asked a man of forty-one who is on no list at all **one question — *how do you know a paper has been read*** — and the answer is the chapter: ***you fold it back wrong. Paper's soft where it's been handled. It's not a mark and it's not a hand and it's not a date.*** He went and did it on the tide in about an hour, with nobody sent him, and reported **three** of the four and **refused to describe the fourth**, and the reason he gave is that nobody asked him. **The rule at the foot of the envelope, read back by the woman who wrote it: *it is not for stopping two people disagreeing. It is for not making a person admit where they keep their papers.*** **The finding on the third of the four is the standing fact: one has been out once and put back, one has been out and put back and out again, one has not been out since it went there, and the fourth has been read and will not be described, and the person holding them is not going to find out.**

19. **A fold is not a chronology, stated on the page by the man whose method it is.** **The instrument's limit, in his own words: *it's not a mark and it's not a hand and it's not a date*** — and on the one copy with two soft lines in it, *there are two soft lines in it and one is older than the other by about a week, and I am telling you that a week is my best estimate and it could be four days, and I am not going to be asked to swear to it and you are not going to write it down.* **A crease is not a date and a fold is not a chronology, and no later batch may use the softness of that paper to establish when anything was read.** What Hettie Brann did was forty seconds long: **the date on the outside of the envelope, in her own hand, in her own ink; four copies back in the four places; nothing altered; nothing read; nothing copied — and she said, in front of about four people: *I have not fixed anything and I want that said in front about four people… one fact is what a season is made of.*** **The versions still disagree and will disagree on the second of March and on the first of April.**

20. **The cost that is not curable, paid in a room on the afternoon of Thursday the fifth of February, in which Milo Rook was not present and was told about it twice in two yards.** **A woman of sixty on the second landing of a building with no number, off every list for nine years and on purpose, is the ninth of the twenty-six names that Mara Quill put into a Crown Heights file on the twelfth of January, and Lena Marr told her first and has not told the other seven.** She asked three questions — *is it in a file, can it be taken out, can I see it* — and the second and the third are the same no, **and the reason they are the same no is the district's one permanent gain**: the ruling that no schedule of persons is to be obtained from this district by that department, in that proceeding or in any proceeding, **is the reason she cannot get a copy of the only paper in the city that has her name on it, and it is forbidden because the district asked for it and Mara Quill drafted the ask.** **Milo Rook's finding, in about four seconds in a yard on the Friday, and it is the fourth time in five weeks he has been the fastest person in a room: *that ruling is doing two jobs — it is protecting two hundred and fifty-two people from a schedule and it is preventing one woman of sixty from finding out what is written about her, and a minute records what a department agreed and not what a department's victory cost.*** **He is not criticising the reporting officer, and he has said in his own book that he is not going to say it in a room for about a year.**

21. **Her twenty-three words, which are on the page and are not to be improved on, and the two things she said after them.** Repeated in a yard on Friday the sixth and not improved, on the condition Lena Marr will repeat them word for word or not at all: ***I have been off every list for nine years and I did it carefully and I did it on purpose and nobody in this building ever asked me why, and that has been the arrangement for nine years, and I would like it recorded that it has been an arrangement and not a difficulty… The file does not bother me. A piece of paper saying I was on a reach in December is not worse than me being sixty and living on a second landing. What bothers me is that I cannot get at it. I cannot see it, I cannot correct it, I cannot come off it, and I cannot ask anybody whether my name is spelled right.*** Then: that Lena Marr is going to come to about nine of these rooms one at a time, **that this is the first, that it is not the same as coming about a form, and that she is the only one of the twenty-six who is going to notice that and it is hers**; and that **Lena Marr is the list** — twenty-six names in a head and in a plastic wallet, deliberately not written down, because a body must not hold a copy of another body's record — *and one day somebody is going to ask you for that list in about four years and you are going to have to decide then whether you are a person or an office.* **Nobody in the district has told the woman of sixty that Mara Quill's price was said out loud in a hall. Lena Marr is the only person who knows all twenty-six and she has not written them down and has said so.**

22. **The batch's answer, said by working people and not by a surveyor, and its price, on Thursday the twelfth of February.** He asked **what do you do when it does not come** in nine places between ten past seven and about four. Eight answers were each the whole of that person's job and none was an answer: *I do the walk. I sweep it. Badly. I fill in the boxes. I print a card. I nail the boards. I open the counter. I put my hand on the corner. I pay for it.* The ninth was given unasked, at about ten past ten, by the man of about twenty-six, who told Milo Rook to write it down and then told him to put his name on it as well: **you stand in it on the day it does not come as well as on the day it does… you cannot tell, from inside a day, whether a thing is going. There is no feeling to it… I have never once known. I just turn up. And if I only turn up for the ones that happen then I have not been keeping anything, I have been collecting.** And the price, in nine words from the same man, given away without knowing: **a blank is not a record of nothing; a blank is a record of a distinction that cannot be made** — *you will not be able to tell which of the nine was a day nobody stood out in and which of the nine was a day there was nothing to see, and I am not going to say it twice.* **This is the batch's answer and it is partial, and the state layer records it as partial and a later batch may not improve it.**

23. **The collision, found on the twelfth of February by a woman of sixty-one at a counter off a card on a wall, and it is now on the page.** **The four pages date the season from the second of January to the fourteenth of April, and the hearing on CO/6/BELL is adjourned to Tuesday the fourteenth of April, and nobody in this city had put the two dates next to each other.** Her words, at about ten past two at the counter, in front of about nine people: *the season ends on the fourteenth of April and there is a hearing in the Hall of Wards on the fourteenth of April, and one of the two is about weather and one of the two is about a sentence somebody took out of a report six years ago, and neither office has told the other that they share a day, and I do not think either of them is going to.* **And it cannot be put in writing by anybody who is allowed to write anything**: the district cannot show an effect upon a person under rule 14, a counter cannot write to a company secretary, and a surveyor would make it into a document with a reference on it and in four years there would be two of them. **No later batch may have either office learn of the other, and no batch may move either date.**

24. **The second weather-driven event, on the afternoon of Thursday the nineteenth of February, in daylight, in front of about nine people, and nothing happened.** Rain off the south-west from about half past one to about ten to five. **The gang was on the back length at half past seven that morning and saw nothing and wrote four words about a cover.** On a hundred and ten yards of frontage that is not a street and cannot be given an address: **fish scales out of a wet stone floor for the first time in nineteen years**, about nine thousand, in the run of a gutter at the north side, confirmed by the man of about forty who has swept that row since he was twenty-one; **coffee at about ten to five, forty minutes early, in rain, with no coffee sold within a hundred and ten yards of that Row since the year before the war, and six people who could not say where it was coming from, and the man it belongs to was not there and has not been asked**; and **seven marks under a rail with six irons on it, measured at about five inches by three, with about an inch of standing water in each, in a run of about three feet, no iron displaced, and no iron absent that anybody can name** — *and there is no list of irons anywhere in this city because nobody has ever needed one, and a thing with no list cannot have a thing taken off it.* **Nobody was hurt, nothing fell down, there is no seam, and there is not one thing in that afternoon that could be put in a box.**

25. **The scale, and the refusal, and it is the cost of the tenth of January paying out.** **He went toward the scale at the north end at about ten to five and slowed down about nine feet short of it and went back up the Row, and nobody saw either of those two things except a woman of sixty who is in a doorway and who is not going to say anything about it.** He did not write down what he saw and the reason is on the page and it is the whole of the January page's consequence: *I have got one sheet of accountancy paper in a building in this city with a name on it, undated and unreasoned, and I am not going to be the reason there are two. If that is the only thing that keeps me off a piece of paper then it is the best thing anybody has done for me in five months and it was done by accident by a foreman writing a rule in grease pencil about not taking a line off.* **No batch may have that page removed from the tin, read by anybody, or told to Ivy Marsden, and a later batch may say the refusal again but may not have him look a second time.**

26. **The second event is not allocated, and the reason is nine words, and it is the same hole as the first of January.** In the river core row there is one hand-entered line and four printed, and the second thing that happened is not in the sheet, because **a row of frontages that is not a street and has no address is not a district and is not a length, and there is no box on that form for it** — *and that is not caution, Mr Rook, that is a man who has one child and a mortgage and about eleven years to go.* **The Row is not a fourth kind of place and has not been converted into one, and it is still a Keepsake remnant and a length is still a third kind of place, and the two remain two kinds of place.** The one row in the district index that might have served was put in by a different department in the autumn in the only box there was and **says a works frontage**, and *a hundred and ten yards of a market is not a works frontage, and nobody is going to have that entry tidied and nobody is going to add a second one.*

27. **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, in six words, and the thirteenth pair.** Thursday the nineteenth of February, in the field, on a wall, at about ten to six, in the rain, with a rule in his hand: > *Weather uses whatever door is open.* The limit written before anything is done with it: *It does not say whose door. It does not say what is behind it. It does not say whether the door was open before today. And I am not going to find out what a door is by shutting one, and I have got one idea in this book about anything and I have had six in five months and every one of them has been about a page, and I am not going to let this one be about a market.* **The book was written nine times between the twenty-ninth of January and the nineteenth of February, on eight dates, and the nineteen-day gap claimed in Chapter 89 was false: the batch itself contains entries on 29 Jan, 30 Jan, 31 Jan, 1 Feb, 2 Feb twice, 5 Feb and 12 Feb. The pair in front of the nineteenth is SEVEN days, off the twelfth. The dated pairs are now THIRTEEN: one six, four threes, one nine, one two, one five, ONE eight, one twenty-five, and three added in February which are a three and two sevens, the three being a fifth three and not one of the four. The four threes are not collapsed, the fifteen-day silence from the fourteenth to the twenty-ninth of January was never written down and a silence is not a pair (Chapter 81's own rule: pairing a silence is how a book becomes a set of numbers), and the single-day interval from the tenth of December is still looked at and is still not written. There is no thirty-six and there is no eleventh pair and there is no twelfth; a later batch may not add one without a dated entry that earns it.** He joins none of them to any other.

28. **The run number, advanced, and not turned into the volume's final image.** **It was looked up in the last week of January, in about four minutes, in a book on a shelf, by a depot clerk of fifty-one who had said she would not and then did, and nobody asked her twice.** It is four characters. **A run number in that authority belongs to a crew and not to a car**, and the working timetable for the week commencing **Sunday the fourteenth of December** carries that crew's number against **two** things on the morning of **Tuesday the sixteenth**: **the 07:52 booked working off Crown Heights, which is the car that went round for five days and two hours, and a depot move to the works at a quarter to eight, which is a light engine and a two-car set going to a gate.** **He wrote both down on the back of the card under the brick, with a date, and did not choose between them, and the line on the front of the card has not been taken off, because a foreman of fifty-four wrote a rule in grease pencil about that in December and it applies to every sheet in this district including a clerk's.** **No destination is named, no passenger's name appears, and the volume's final image — a recovered timetable containing a destination that does not exist with the passengers' names real in it — is not written in this batch and may not be written by a later batch without the outline.** The card has been under the brick since the twenty-first of January and was still under it on the twentieth of February, and about four times a day somebody reads it and nobody moves it.

29. **The company secretary's letter, four paragraphs, and what it does and does not do.** It came on **Tuesday the seventeenth of February** and answers Mara Quill's nine-minute letter of the sixteenth of January. Three refusals, all correct: a return is a record of work and not a document of proceedings; a copy may be supplied to a reporting officer in proceedings and not to a party; **and the officer's election is not a facility the authority is able to extend to an authority that is not a party to the proceeding in which the document is sought.** The fourth paragraph is the one that moves something: *the authority confirms that the return for the current year is filed under its own reference and bears the date of completion. The authority does not treat this paragraph as a disclosure of the contents of the document and the request is accordingly refused in full.* **A reference and a date and no copy. It confirms the return exists and is complete and counter-signed and says in writing that it is not disclosing what is on it, and the sixth line of the fourteenth of January, fifty-four words, in a clerk's own hand, is not on the page and Milo Rook does not know it and is not told it in this batch.** The district's position is unchanged, there is no copy, and the standing figure for it is the seventh door again in a different building.

30. **The relationship, milestone 2, and it is a working instrument and it is not warm.** **There is no kiss, no declaration, no jealousy scene and no rescue in these ten chapters, and the Lark Street business is not raised in any of them.** What is on the page between them is a corridor on Friday the twentieth of February and about eleven words each way, and the trust is procedural: he tells her he has not crossed and the tally is nine; **she says out loud, in a corridor, that in about two months the first weather-driven event anybody allocates will be on a printed sheet and there will be a great deal said in offices about what the Atlas can do in a season, and none of it will be true, and the truest thing about the winter happened because two women in this district put seven days into a counter and a satchel on their own signatures, and one of them is a woman of sixty-one at a clinic counter who is not going to be thanked and one of them is going to be leaned on.** **Mara Quill has written a second letter, on the eighteenth, and has not sent it, and has written down in advance that she is not going to and will read it back on the day, and has said in a corridor with a clerk within about thirty feet, for the second time in a month and on purpose, that she is not going to ask him about the seventh of January.** **He has written in his own book that he did not ask her which of the four copies is in a flat, and that not asking was the correct procedure and not a courtesy, and that in December he watched her look at a table for about four seconds and wrote down something else about it.** **Her custody register page is still in a drawer with a coat in it, is not produced, is not confessed to, and her knowledge of the token and the custody column is still unused and undeclared, and he still does not know she refused anything.** The **fourth copy** of the return route has been read and will not be described, and neither he nor the reader is told where it is.

31. **Milo's orientation ledger, and it did not move.** **The remembered-route list stands at TEN, the original eight intact, none removed, all ten marked by somebody who is not him, nothing added and nothing lost in these ten chapters, and the ninth crossing's failure to cost one is still unexplained and is not explained by anything here.** **He crossed nothing in Chapters 81–90.** The written line home went on the twenty-fourth of November, which is day one, and the figures on the page are **the sixty-seventh day on the twenty-ninth of January, the sixty-eighth on the thirtieth, the sixty-ninth on the thirty-first, the seventieth on the first of February, the seventy-first on the second (twice), the seventy-fourth on the fifth, the eighty-first on the twelfth, the eighty-eighth on the nineteenth and the eighty-ninth on the twentieth** — **blank throughout, with three marks in three hands, and nobody has asked anybody to fill it in since the twenty-seventh of December, and the district's not asking is recorded on the page as a refusal and not as a kindness**: *a thing everybody has quietly agreed not to ask about is a thing that has quietly become nobody's, and this district has lost two rooms and a school place and a grave in six years to things nobody asked about.* **Iona Sen's tally is unchanged at nine lines, the paper is Hettie Brann's and the pocket is Dr Sen's, and he has not looked at it since the thirteenth of January.**

32. **The Stage 2 cost, its third instance, and what it is and is not.** A **taste**, on the Saturday the thirty-first at about a quarter to three: nine cups out of the same tin and the same pot in one day and at the ninth he put the cup down, **because he had wanted to know whether he had been awake since about seven in the morning and had used taste as an instrument and the instrument was not there.** He used a watch instead. **It is a sound and a taste and no face and no place and no person and no name, and he does not know whether that is one thing or two, and he has still decided nothing.** **The first personal blank, the seam-scar, the record blank and the authorial blank appear nowhere in Chapters 81–90 and may not.**

33. **Stage 2 continues and does not complete, and he holds nothing and goes in nowhere.** **There is no stabilisation in these ten chapters.** He did not go into the Row, did not go into the length, held nothing on the wall, and did not cross. The reason is on the page twice: that he is not going to be a man who turns a length into a wound because he has learned a new thing about wounds, and that **getting better at hiding a thing is not the same as being right.** **A more advanced grammar remains useless without patient observation, and this batch's deepening of the identification is the Row's own behaviour in the rain, which nobody can classify and which is a Keepsake remnant doing something a remnant has not been on the page doing before.**

34. **The fourth sighting of the man in the works jacket, and the refusal, and the rules.** **On the afternoon of Thursday the nineteenth of February, at about ten past two, about forty yards off the north end, in a doorway out of the rain, notebook out, he wrote once and did not come down the Row.** That is four sightings: two in December, one on Monday the fifth of January, and this one. **He was not there on Thursday the fifth of February, which was a walk day, and Pell Harrow was on the round and saw him not be there. Nobody followed him and nobody has.** He remains unidentified and unresolved. **Pell's sentence, in a yard: *four sightings is not a pattern, two cards on one brick is not a pattern, and if I put a line under any of them I will have made a column out of a doorway and a book on a shelf, and a column is a case, and I have watched two men in this district make one and I am not going to be the third.*** **There are three things under the brick on the trestle table: the run-number card with two lines on the back of it, the jacket card, and Pell Harrow's own blank card, which is blank and has been since the nineteenth of January and will be blank on the second of March.**

35. **Pell Harrow, and Tern, and the standing refusal.** He is on the round, out at eight and in at four on a working day, and in the seven days of the last week of January out at eight and in at seven. **He went up the south for twenty-two days, from Sunday the twenty-eighth of December to Monday the nineteenth of January, and nothing has been asked him and nothing has been said by him in these ten chapters, and he has said in a yard that nobody has asked him and nobody is going to, and that a man is going to read his card one day and it is going to say nothing, and that he decided that up the south.** **Tern is not found. The list in the tin is not a finding, Ostrow confirms nothing, and no chapter in this block rescued anybody or asked anything.** He is not Milo's assistant and he is not on any of the forms.

36. **The prices of Volume 01 and of Batch 0001 carried into this batch and not one of them moved, and this is stated once, in Chapter 90's schedule list, and the state layer records what the batch added.** **A hundred and forty feet of named frontage behind a fence with a settlement plate and a date on it and a gang on a fortnightly inspection. A man asleep on a third floor of a building with no number, not written down and not woken. A man of forty-one behind a boatyard on no list at all. A mechanic of sixty-one on a drainage record forever who will not swear. A conductor of thirty in a printed rule book whose own hearing has no date. A woman of fifty-four with a plot number and no bill in six years. Two children lost in October and still lost. Two and four a head a week, in advance, week by week, with no term, for two hundred and fifty-two people in a building with no number. A woman of thirty-one with a two-year note who has a page in a drawer with a coat in it that she has not used and is not going to. And one reference — this batch's addition, declared — to a woman of sixty-eight on nothing, which is the fifth across five chapters of Volume 02 and the first in these ten.** **The two money totals are not printed in these ten chapters and the multiplication is still never shown. Roy Pryce's nine words in the hours column are still nine words and are not re-counted anywhere in these ten chapters.**

37. **The standing answer, and the standing question, and neither is closed.** The answer: **a length is a third kind of place and a Row of frontages is a Keepsake remnant and they are two kinds of place and are not merged; the writing down is what lets a department find a place and the walking is what makes it a place; the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything; and the weather-driven season is kept, if it is kept at all, by turning up on the days it does not come.** The question: **why a region the Registry enters as stable appears on a forecast of weather-driven seam events at all, when the only fact about it in this city is that nobody has ever been there to write down what happens in it.** **Three nils in nine weeks, a man of fifty-eight with a rod in the summer, a row of districts printed from a list a hundred and forty years old that has never been printed from a list of offices, eleven events with ten of them unallocated and allocated on the day, and no form on the earth for a place that people use and that no department can enter.**

38. **No new grammar, faction, antagonist, final enemy, or fourth kind of place.** The grammars in use in these ten chapters are **Keepsake** (the Row in the rain) and the block names no new dominant grammar. **A length is still a third kind of place and is not a remnant, not a failure state, and not a form of any of the seven grammars. The Row is still a Keepsake remnant and has not been converted into a fourth category, and the hole in the paperwork has not been turned into one.** **Sel Iri does not appear. Halden Vey does not appear and is not named. Orrin Sable does not appear. Tarin Kest does not appear. Ivo Sarratt is not named in these ten chapters. Amos Trench, Bettina Sallow and Thread Hall do not appear and the withdrawal file is not touched. Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–90.** The antagonists of this block are **a printed form with five rows, a line under the words *officer responsible*, a machine that prints a sentence where there used to be a dash, a column headed *scheduled*, and a letter from a company secretary that is entirely correct** — and **not one new face.**

## What Chapters 91–100 fixed on the page, in the same form as CR-013

Each item below is now prose rather than plan, and each is load-bearing in a way a later batch could break without intending to. **None of them changes a premise, a world rule, a power rule, the antagonist ladder, or the planned ending. Every figure here is quotable from a chapter in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0091.md` … `chapter-0100.md`, and this list was written from the manuscript, with two stated carried figures that are arithmetic and are marked as such where they occur: the second tin at **ten, eleven, twelve, thirteen and fourteen** weeks, none of which is printed in the chapter that owns the day, the printed figures being **fifteen** (Ch 99) and **sixteen** (Ch 100, written in advance for Friday the third of April).** Chapters 91–100 are the **fourth block of Volume 02** — the second month of the season and the fortnight before the fourteenth — and they inherit the Chapters 81–90, 71–80, 61–70 and Volume 01 lists above whole.

1. **The batch's shape and its boundary.** Ten chapters, **Saturday the twenty-first of February to Wednesday the first of April, forty days.** It includes the second time four copies of the return-route card are compared, **on Friday the sixth of March and not on Monday the second**, and **the first of April**, the last time before the fourteenth. The second comparison is four days off the first working day of March; Chapter 92 says so in the sentence that opens the scene, and the rule as fixed on the page in Chapter 100 is once a month on a day the holder fixes herself. The three dates are **the second of February, the sixth of March and the first of April**. **The volume's immediate promise was answered in Batch 0001 and is not re-opened; the hearing was answered in Batch 0002 and is not re-opened.** **This batch does not resolve the volume and it does not write the volume's final image.** The run number on the card under a brick is **advanced one step and stopped**, and what it advanced to is registered at item 18.

2. **The weekday chain, extended and not re-derived.** From Batch 0003, which left the chain at 20th Fri: **21st Sat, 22nd Sun, 23rd Mon, 24th Tue, 25th Wed, 26th Thu, 27th Fri, 28th Sat, 1st March Sun, 2nd March Mon, 3rd Tue, 4th Wed, 5th Thu, 6th Fri, 7th Sat, 8th Sun, 9th Mon, 10th Tue, 11th Wed, 12th Thu, 13th Fri, 14th Sat, 15th Sun, 16th Mon, 17th Tue, 18th Wed, 19th Thu, 20th Fri, 21st Sat, 22nd Sun, 23rd Mon, 24th Tue, 25th Wed, 26th Thu, 27th Fri, 28th Sat, 29th Sun, 30th Mon, 31st Tue, 1st April Wed, 2nd Thu, 3rd Fri**, and the fixed point **14th April Tue.** **A later batch may not re-derive a weekday and may not move the second of March, the first of April or the fourteenth of April.** **The chapter dates, all on the page: 21–23 Feb (Ch 91, Sat–Mon), 28 Feb–6 Mar (Ch 92), 11 Mar Wed (Ch 93), 14–15 Mar (Ch 94, Sat–Sun), 16–19 Mar (Ch 95), 22 Mar Sun (Ch 96), 26 Mar Fri (Ch 97), 30 Mar Mon (Ch 98), 31 Mar Tue (Ch 99), 1 Apr Wed (Ch 100).**

3. **The gang's week, the walks, the racks, and the second tin's arithmetic, which is the count most likely to be got wrong.** The gang is on the back length **Mondays and Thursdays at 07.30**, the weekly sheet covers Monday to Sunday, it goes into the rack on the Friday, the duplicate into a tin, and the duplicate into the second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room of the Civic Atlas Office under the repair schedule. **In this block the walks are the 23rd and 26th of February, the 2nd, 5th, 9th, 12th, 16th, 19th, 23rd, 26th and 30th of March, and the sheets go into the rack on the 27th of February, the 6th, 13th, 20th and 27th of March, and the 3rd of April — six Fridays, and the thirty-first of March is a Tuesday and is not one of them.** **The second tin held NINE weeks of gang carbon plus the one page that is not a gang sheet on Friday the twentieth of February; TEN on the twenty-seventh of February; ELEVEN on the sixth of March; TWELVE on the thirteenth; THIRTEEN on the twentieth; FOURTEEN on the twenty-seventh; FIFTEEN on Friday the twenty-seventh, which is printed in Chapter 99; and SIXTEEN on Friday the third of April, which is written in advance and printed in Chapter 100.** **The reason column is empty on every walk in these ten chapters and that is correct. No new condition-column entry is printed in this block, and the last printed one remains *Cover reset. Rocking.* (Chapters 85 and 88), and the cover is still rocking: **seven weeks and two months, eleven men, twice a week, and there is now no form in the city that can take a sentence about it** (item 12).** All of the eleven, twelve, thirteen and fourteen figures are carried arithmetic and are not quotable from a chapter.

4. **The first two blanks, and the first wrong-shaped instrument in the batch, and it belongs to the district and not to the surveyor.** **The second side of the sheet of who does what and on which day is a week, and the four lines on it as at the twentieth of February were on Monday the second, Thursday the fifth, Thursday the twelfth and Thursday the nineteenth — that is, on Mondays and Thursdays — and the reason is that the round is a working day and the sheet travels with the round, and the gang is on a Monday and a Thursday.** The fishmonger calls the lot prices at about ten past five every day; the sweeper sweeps at about ten past six every day; the coffee man puts his hand on a corner at about half past five every day and has done for nineteen years. **A blank on that side is a day when nobody stood in the weather, and the side has been blank on five days in seven since the second of February.** **Saturday the twenty-first and Sunday the twenty-second of February are the first two blanks, and the reason there is nothing on them is that the round does not go out at a weekend and the sheet is not carried on a Saturday.** The finding, in his own hand: *the district has been telling the truth about a person and lying about a day, and it has been doing it with the best rule anybody has written in four months.* **Rue's own sentence, and it is her rule and not his: *I did not think about it. I have been thinking about it for nine days and I did not think about it in eleven minutes.*** **A later batch may not turn the second side into a page about people and may not add a column about why a day is blank** — that is the fourth of his, item 11 — **and may not cure the five-days-in-seven problem by having Milo Rook write a form about it, which he did not do and said so in a yard.**

5. **The SEAM EVENT ALLOCATION sheet, its fourth column, and the two refusals of it, which are the best sentences a works clerk has said in this volume.** The sheets are weekly, printed on the Friday for a week commencing the following Monday, **and the forecast column is still hand-transferred with a pen by a man of about fifty, fifty-one next month, nineteen years in the room.** **There have been five of them since the twenty-sixth of January and he has written the same five figures out five times and has not reduced the four in the river core and has not reduced the zero against Mireward, and the river core row still carries one hand-entered line.** **The fourth column is headed *ALLOCATED TO* and in nineteen years and two runs of this season no person has ever filled it in.** The distinction, in eleven words: **the box under *officer responsible* is a promise that somebody is on the other end of it, and the box under *allocated to* is a record of the promise being kept, and it is the only one of the four columns on that printed side that is about a person doing an act, and the act is handing somebody a piece of paper on a day, and there is no day.** Three names went in off the wall board that has never once been wrong about a building, unasked and untold. **Mireward's was refused in twenty words and it is a different refusal from January's**: *there is nobody to hand it to… if his name is on a piece of paper then somebody in about four years is going to ask Percy why Mireward was not allocated in the first week of March, and Percy is fifty-eight and has a bad knee in the second week of every March, and then somebody in a room has written down that Mireward has no officer responsible, and I have just done the January thing to him in a different column and got further with it.* **The river core box was left blank and the reason is not a principle**: *Dilley Farr is in this building, thirty-one years, on the second of the two tables under the north window, and walks to about eleven o'clock for a cup of tea, and a man of about fifty has watched that for nineteen years, and a name in that box is a claim that he was handed a piece of paper about a seam event on a day, and there is no day.* **And the cost, seven days later, on Friday the sixth of March: a printed district returns card comes back with a sentence in the reason column where it has always had the machine's dash — *allocation sheets w/e XXIII and II, allocated-to column uncompleted* — and the finding about machinery is: *a machine is a much worse thing than a man about being difficult, because a man stops and a machine does not.*** **Mireward carries no new nil in this block: the three nils stand and this batch does not make a fourth. The sheet gains no row. The forecast column is still not typed.**

6. **The number on the outside of the envelope, and the finding of the first of April, and it is the batch's best instrument.** Hettie Brann, the holder since the nineteenth of January, compares version and date and nothing else once a month, in the half hour between one and half past one that the counter is shut, on a day she fixes herself and writes on the envelope. **She has now done it three times: the second of February, which was the first working day of that month, the sixth of March, which is four days off the Monday she had it written down for, and the first of April. March and April were not first working days and the rule as fixed on the page in Chapter 100 is once a month, on a day she fixes herself and writes on the envelope. There is no fourth before the fourteenth and there is no fourth after it, because after the fourteenth this volume's business is in a hall and not at a counter, and she has not been asked to invent one.** Three of the four copies say version one and one says version two, and the fourth has been read and will not be described, and she is not going to find out. **From the sixth of March she has written a count on the outside of the envelope in pencil in her own hand, and it reads *1 of 4*, and it is the only number about four people she is allowed to have.** **On the first of April the count is identical — *1 of 4. — 1.iv.* — and the fact behind it is different, because the odd one out is a different piece of paper in a different building and she cannot tell which, and the rule at the foot does not fire because the hand is the same on all four.** *If anybody in four years reads *1 of 4* on this corner they are going to think they know which month it was written in, and they will be wrong.* **And she refuses to write *and it is a different one*, in these words: *a number is the one thing about four people that I am allowed to have, and it is the one thing I am going to have.*** **A later batch may not resolve the disagreement between the four copies, may not have her find the fourth, and may not have the number change.**

7. **A working day with a person in it, and the person is Nell Crale, and it is not in a yard.** Sixty-three, **Bridge Street, low end, four rooms over a shop with a cellar under it**, **twenty-two years of a milk-and-ice round in two baskets on a hand barrow, six stops, forty-one pounds**, and no machine on that street because a continuity order of six years back and a temporary route and a fenced hundred and forty feet of frontage and about nine other things took them away and were replaced by nothing. **The stops, in order: a corner shop that has had no ice since about 1971 and has never said so; the cellar, six inches of silt with salt in it and a frost that has not gone out in nine years, the works' answer is November and nobody came in the March either and she has stopped saying so; a door with a step; and a house of a woman of seventy-nine who does not get up before eight and does not go on a Friday because her daughter comes at four and not on a Friday.** Two sentences of hers that are the chapter and are not to be improved on: **grit in an axle is a low place and it is not a roof and it is not a measurement and it is not on any form and you cannot buy one**;
