The Lark Street grievance is not named, not resolved, not softened and not referred to in any of these ten chapters.** **Mara Quill appears three times, all of them work: a corridor at ten past ten on the fourteenth, a room at about half past one on the fourteenth, and a corridor at half past twelve on the twenty-third.** **Her custody register page is untouched for the fourth time, for the fourth consecutive block: it is in a drawer with a coat in it, and it was not produced, not named, not confessed to, and not mentioned in dialogue in any of the ten chapters, and he did not ask, and she did not offer it, and the reader is given nothing new and spends nothing.** **What is new is the test he set himself, and it has been taken and he has failed it. On the thirty-first of March he wrote that he did not tell her about the season and that not telling her was not the correct procedure and not a courtesy, and that if he went on not telling her after the fourteenth of April then it was not a rule. On the fourteenth he did not tell her in a corridor at ten past ten, or in a room at about eleven, or at about half past one when a line with a condition in it was read out. He wrote it down at the top of an entry on the twenty-first of April, in the imperative, and has written in Chapter 110 that a thing you do quietly is a thing that is going to get done, which is a woman of about fifty-four's sentence from a yard in this city in the middle of the winter. He has not told her since, and has written that being right about it four times is not the same as being a person somebody can plan a Tuesday with.** **He has also not asked her about the seventh of January: that is five times in a fortnight, and he has stopped keeping a column of it and started keeping a number, and a number is a column, and he has written that the not-asking has stopped being procedure and become something else and that he has not decided what.** **What she gave him is procedural and practical and has an edge: *do not put a number on it, do not put a reference on it, and do not improve it afterwards in a yard, because I will be in that yard* — and *I would be able to, and that is not a threat, it is a statement about what an advocate is.* A later batch may not have her hand the custody register page over as a favour, may not have her confess unless the scene earns it at a cost, and he must not ask.

24. **No new grammar, faction, antagonist, final enemy, or fourth kind of place.** **The grammars in use in these ten chapters are Keepsake (the Row and its Wednesday) and the block names no new dominant grammar. A length is still a third kind of place and is not a remnant, not a failure state, and not a form of any of the seven grammars. The Row is still a Keepsake remnant. A settlement plate is a plate, and a fence is a fence, and a hand index of cards for rateable things is an instrument, and a departmental list, a minute, an allocation sheet, a return and a working timetable are not kinds of place and none of them has been described as one.** **Sel Iri does not appear. Halden Vey does not appear and is not named. Orrin Sable does not appear. Tarin Kest does not appear. Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–110. The man in the works jacket is not seen once in these ten chapters, nobody was asked to look, and no fifth sighting and no fifth refusal was made by anybody.** **The three clues from Volume 01 appear in no chapter of this block as a document and are joined to nothing, and no fifth refusal to join them was made by anybody. Two documents still carry a pair of marks and not three; three references exist and are references and not pairs; the twenty-first's Registry certification is drawn where the two confirmed marks are pressed, a difference in method and not in age; Kest has not matched the strings; and a fold is still not a chronology — no document's age in this block is established by a mark on it, and Hettie Brann's count is a count of versions and not a date of reading.** **The three things under the brick are still three.** **The office of the First Surveyor is named three more times — on the third matter, on the fourth, and in the reason the plate cannot be reported — and there is still no person behind it, and the batch does not make it request, receive or act on anything.** The one thing built for one purpose and found the wrong shape for another, in this block, is **a hand index: it is a card for a rateable thing with a street on it, and it is asked to find a length, a row, a fence and a woman.**

25. **Pell Harrow, and Tern, and the standing refusal, unchanged and unasked.** **He is on the round, out at eight and in at four, and he is not named in any of these ten chapters.** **Tern is still not found, the list in the tin is not a finding, Ostrow confirms nothing, and no chapter in this block rescued anybody or asked anything about the twenty-two days up the south.** **His card under the brick is blank and has been since the nineteenth of January and he has not taken it and nobody has.** The standing rule about that brick — *a card under a brick is a thing and not a job, and about four times a day somebody looks at a job and decides it is not theirs* — **was acted on in Chapter 110 and not by him and not by Milo Rook, and the person who acted on it was a man of forty-one on no list at all, and the rule held.** **He has no power, his mark still holds for a working shift and not overnight, and it was not used and is not going to be.**

26. **Volume 01's people carry Volume 01's prices into this block and not one of them is refunded, and this batch adds exactly two declared references and no more.** **A woman of sixty-eight on nothing** — **this block's addition, declared: two references, in Chapter 103's Field Book and in Chapter 110's carried list, and they are the seventh and the eighth across eight chapters of Volume 02, and the block's own count says so, and nobody went up three flights of stairs about her and she is not thanked, argued back, put on anything or solved, and her name is not written once in Chapters 101–110.** **The woman on the fourth floor of Cedar Court is not mentioned in these ten chapters at all, so that the two sixty-eights cannot be folded, and the woman of sixty on the second landing of a building with no number is a different and unnamed resident and is the ninth of the twenty-six names and is not named either.** Old Mabb is a clause in Chapter 110's list and is not named and is not woken. Ivy Marsden is not named, does not appear and is not told anything; the page with her name struck through it is not read, not removed and not mentioned in any of the ten chapters, and **Milo Rook did not go near the north end of Saltmarket Row in this block and has written down that he did not and has not written down why.** **A hundred and forty feet of named frontage behind a fence with a settlement plate on it, and the plate is in the grass.** A conductor of thirty in a printed rule book whose own hearing still has no date. A woman of fifty-four with a plot number and no bill in six years. Two children lost in October and still lost. A mechanic of sixty-one on a drainage record forever who will not swear. A man asleep on a third floor. **Owen Fitch's name is still on five copies of a sheet of paper with a floor that is not his, and it has not been taken off any of them.** **Roy Pryce's reason in the hours column is still nine words and is not re-counted anywhere in these ten chapters.** **The two money totals, five hundred and fifty-four pounds and forty and forty-three pounds and two, are carried forward unchanged, are not printed in these ten chapters, and the multiplication is still never shown, because the two are inconsistent at the shilling and that is inherited from Chapter 38.**

27. **The pressures, and the shape of the batch, stated for a later writer.** The batch is **the resumed hearing and the volume's answer, built on eleven days of narrowing and a week of widening.** It contains **one working day with a person in it** (Ch 104, Bettina Sallow, Thursday the sixteenth), **one field or boundary pair** (Ch 101, the fence and the plate at the low end, and the second side and its Wednesday), **one failure** (Ch 105), **one changed method and one recovery by the same person** (Ch 106, Hettie Brann, whose whole act is a counter and a rule she wrote in January), **one refusal that is correct** (Ch 104, the works clerk's *nobody sweeps this room*; Ch 101, all four doors; Ch 108, the district cannot ask who is on item three), **one cost that is not curable paid in a room** (Ch 103), **the answer in working people's mouths** (Ch 107, the fishmonger, nine words), **an escalation nobody expected** (Ch 108, the second list), **and the standing question** (Ch 110). **Five moments in which a thing somebody built for one purpose turned out to be the wrong shape for another:** a settlement plate built to record a certification, with no nail to report it off (Ch 101); a gang sheet's condition column, with no way to record a fault that stopped (Ch 101); a hand index of cards for rateable things, asked to find a length and a fence (Ch 104); a query box that had stopped asking about a sentence and had started asking about a box, which is not a safer question (Ch 104); and a departmental list, which gave a district a chair in March and took it away in April with no notice served on anybody (Ch 108). **The block's boundary is honest: the order is not discharged, the name is not on the street index, the adjournment is not moved and the matter is not re-adjourned, the removed sentence is not restored, the fifth sighting and the fifth refusal are both not made, a crossing is not required and not made, nobody goes to Mireward, there is no fourth nil, and the volume's final image is not written — and Milo Rook does not end it as the author of the back length's maintenance, and he has not been for seven months, and the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything, and that is a list of about fourteen other people and not one of them has been asked to be on it.**

## What Chapters 111–120 fixed on the page, in the same form as CR-013

Each item below is now prose rather than plan, and each is load-bearing in a way a later batch could break without intending to. **None of them changes a premise, a world rule, a power rule, the antagonist ladder, or the planned ending. Every figure here was made from the manuscript and is quotable from a chapter in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0111.md` … `chapter-0120.md`, after the writer's own weekday, day-count, pair-count, word-count, elapsed-time, counter-sequence and meta-language pass.** Chapters 111–120 are the **sixth block of Volume 02** — the penultimate block, the second half of the volume's last movement — and they inherit the Chapters 101–110, 91–100, 81–90, 71–80, 61–70 and Volume 01 lists above whole.

1. **The batch's shape and its boundary.** Ten chapters, **Sunday the twenty-sixth of April to Thursday the twenty-first of May, twenty-six days**, and it includes **Monday the fourth of May** and **Tuesday the fifth of May**. **The volume's immediate promise was answered in Batch 0001, the January hearing in Batch 0002 and the resumed hearing in Batch 0005, and none of the three is re-opened. This batch does not resolve the volume and it does not write the volume's final image, which is in Chapter 130 and one batch away. The run number on the card under a brick HAS NOT MOVED AT ALL in this block, and the reason is on the page in Chapter 118: a rule holds by not being tested.**

2. **The weekday chain, extended and not re-derived, and one inherited error in the state layer corrected at the chapter.** From Batch 0005, which left the chain at **25 April Sat**: **26th Sun, 27th Mon, 28th Tue, 29th Wed, 30th Thu, 1st May Fri, 2nd May Sat, 3rd May Sun, 4th May Mon, 5th May Tue, 6th May Wed, 7th May Thu, 8th May Fri, 9th Sat, 10th Sun, 11th Mon, 12th Tue, 13th Wed, 14th Thu, 15th Fri, 16th Sat, 17th Sun, 18th Mon, 19th Tue, 20th Wed, 21st Thu**, and then **22nd Fri, 23rd Sat, 24th Sun, 25th Mon, 26th Tue, 27th Wed, 28th Thu, 29th Fri, 30th Sat, 31st Sun.** **THE THIRD OF MAY IS A SUNDAY.** `state/current.md` and CR-031 through CR-035 all said *Monday the third of May* for the fourth comparison; that was a weekday error in the state layer and in the Batch 0005 brief, and **the rule at the foot of the envelope says the comparison happens on the FIRST WORKING DAY OF THE MONTH, which is Monday the fourth**, and **Chapter 118 states the correction on the page in Milo's own voice — *the third of May is a Sunday, the first working day of the month is the fourth, which is a Monday, and a man of thirty-one has had a note in a book in a coat since January that said the third, and the third is a Sunday, and he has had it wrong on paper for four months and has now got it right in a book in a coat, which is the only place in this district where anything gets corrected, and he has not told anybody, and nobody asked.*** **The chapter wins, per CR-023, and a later batch may not move either date.** The business of **Tuesday the fifth of May** is fixed and is a Tuesday. **The chapter dates, all on the page: 26–27 April (Ch 111, Sun–Mon), 28 April (Ch 112, Tue), 29–30 April (Ch 113, Wed–Thu), 1 May (Ch 114, Fri), 2 May (Ch 115, Sat), 3–4 May (Ch 116, Sun–Mon a.m.), 4 May p.m. (Ch 117, Mon), 4 May p.m. and 5 May (Ch 118), 6–8 May (Ch 119, Wed–Fri), 8–21 May (Ch 120).**

3. **The gang's week, the walks, the racks, and the second tin's arithmetic.** The gang is on the back length **Mondays and Thursdays at 07.30**, the weekly sheet covers Monday to Sunday, it goes into the rack on the Friday, the duplicate into a tin, and the duplicate into the second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room of the Civic Atlas Office under a repair schedule. **The walks in this block are the 27th and 30th of April and the 4th, 7th, 11th, 14th, 18th and 21st of May — EIGHT WALKS, ALL EIGHT OF THEM INSIDE THE BLOCK'S OWN TWENTY-SIX DAYS, and the next walk after the twenty-first of May is Monday the twenty-fifth, which belongs to the next block. The rack days in this block are the 1st, 8th and 15th of May — three Fridays; the fourth is the twenty-second and belongs to the next block.** **The second tin held NINETEEN weeks of carbon plus the one page that is not a gang sheet on Friday the twenty-fourth of April (inherited, printed in Chapter 109), TWENTY on Friday the first of May, TWENTY-ONE on Friday the eighth of May and TWENTY-TWO on Friday the fifteenth of May, and those four figures are each printed in the chapter that owns the day.** **The condition column is empty on every walk in this block: eight walks in April (the seventh is the twenty-seventh, printed in Chapter 111, and the eighth is the thirtieth) and six in May (the first is the fourth, printed in Chapter 117), fourteen since the first of April, printed in Chapter 120. The reason column is empty on all of them and that is correct, and no column on that sheet wants to know why a column is empty. A later batch may not reduce any of these figures, may not put a word in the condition column about why, and may not turn the empty walks into a record of anything.**

4. **A season ending is a column that stops being printed, and it is the batch's first finding, and it is said out loud in a yard.** **Monday the twenty-seventh of April at about a quarter to ten, in about forty seconds, in a yard of about nine people, by Milo Rook, and nobody wrote it down — the thirteenth time in fourteen weeks.** *It is the first Monday since the second of January that this district has treated as an ordinary Monday, and the reason is not that nothing happened on it. The reason is that the machinery is still printing and the chair is not. A district that is waiting for a chair is a district with a date. A district with no date has to be in the street in the morning, because in the street in the morning is the only thing it has got, and it is not nothing, and it is eleven weeks of it.* And: ***A season ending is not an event. A season ending is a column that stops being printed.*** And: ***a district that cannot produce a document is a district that cannot be instructed, and a district that cannot be instructed has to be in the street in the morning.*** **A later batch may not improve any of those three sentences and may not put a next to any of them.**

5. **A column that has been empty for eleven weeks is not an empty column; it is a column nobody reads. Roy Pryce's second and a half is the whole of it.** **Monday the twenty-seventh of April, at about ten past ten, four sentences and a correction, unasked by anybody and asked once:** *The last walk anything went into that column was the twelfth of February and I have not read it once since, and I did not not-read it. **Reading it and not reading it are two different things and the difference is about a second and a half and the second and a half is not a decision anybody makes.*** And then the second half, which is new: somebody will fill it in one day on a Tuesday in a wet week and it will be wrong, and in about four years somebody will read a sheet and see a fault on a Tuesday in May and a gap from February to May and be perfectly entitled to say that nobody was watching, and **there is no column on this sheet that wants to know why a column is empty and I am not going to build one, and I have not built one in eleven weeks and I have had about four times in eleven weeks when I wanted to, and I did not, and there is no rule.** A later batch may not put a word in that column, may not have anybody look at it, and may not convert the second and a half into a rule.

6. **The second side has SEVEN blank Wednesdays in it by the twenty-first of May and the sheet is right about every one of them, and about nine people in eleven streets stood in the weather on ONE of them and for nothing at all — the fifteenth of April, which is the fourth thing in five months that cannot be shown to anybody, and a blank that is right about a day it cannot see is not a fifth thing.** **REPAIRED AT CR-040 ITEM 1. The batch as first written multiplied the fifteenth into five and said the district stood in the weather on all of them; that is a break with Chapter 109 line 45, which fixes exactly four unshowable things in five months and names the fifteenth of April alone, and with Chapters 95 and 96, which establish that the sheet is right on Wednesdays because the only person standing in the weather is the man of about sixty at a shed at Cardew Docks.** The eighth, fifteenth and twenty-second of April are named in Chapter 111 with a fourth coming on the twenty-ninth; the fifth, the sixth of May, in Chapter 119; and the sixth and seventh, the thirteenth and twentieth of May, are in Chapter 120. A blank on that side is a day when nobody stood in the weather; the sheet is right; there is no column on it for a man who is somewhere else; the man of about sixty is at a shed at Cardew Docks on all seven and does not know a piece of paper exists and is not going to be told; and the twenty-eight words the man of about forty put under a rule in March are undated and uninitialled and have been read by him and acted on by nobody. **A later batch may not strike one out, may not fill one, may not ask the man of about forty about them, may not tell the man of about sixty, may not have anybody stand in the weather on a Wednesday, and may not turn the fifteenth of April into a series.**

7. **The failure, the sixth, the same one, and the person who names the shape is not a clerk and is the person the form is about.** **Wednesday the twenty-ninth of April, a kitchen table, a cold cup of tea, accountancy paper headed in his own hand *OCCUPANCY — RESIDENTS, TEMPORARY ACCOMMODATION*, the six columns in the order of the minute and a seventh headed *why this person is not on the district's schedule* — and he got ONE line, not two, and about a hundred and ten words, and every word of it is true, and it has no name in it because he does not know her name and the file does not and exactly one person in this city does.** The line: *9. Second landing. One resident, woman, sixty, present since the fifteenth of December past. Named rateable head: Marr, L. On the district's schedule: no. **A department made her rateable by a schedule of persons that this district has refused to supply since the fourth of December, and the refusal is the district's and it is minuted, and the paragraph that stops her is the one the named rateable head drafted herself, and the answer to why she is not on a schedule is a minute in another building that says a department shall not obtain one from this district while that answer stands.*** **Thursday the thirtieth of April at about half past eight, in a doorway, in nine seconds, off one sentence — Lena Marr, about thirty-four, who is not a clerk and has never been one: *you have written a seventh column*** — and then four things, all on the page: that she knows what it is about and is not going to ask and he is not going to tell her and the two are the same decision; that she has twenty-six names and has not written them down, on purpose, for about four years; that he has spent seven months finding that out about a plate in a field and has never once applied it to a piece of paper in his own coat; and the sentence below. **A later batch may not have anybody else read that form, may not have the woman of sixty named, may not make the woman of sixty be in a room, and may not let the seventh column acquire a second line.**

8. **A HEAD IS THE ONLY REPOSITORY IN THIS CITY THAT CANNOT BE PRODUCED AGAINST ANYBODY, and it is Lena Marr's, it is in a doorway, it is the largest single idea in this block, and it is the only one of the block's ideas that came from a party.** *You can send for a tin. You can send for a counter. You can send for a coat and a man in it. You cannot send for what I am thinking about at eleven o'clock at night, and that is not because it is hidden, it is because it was never written, and a thing that was never written is the only kind of thing in this city that nobody can take off me, including the twenty-six, including the woman on the second landing, including the one I drafted the paragraph for on the fourth of December past.* And the price, once: **she has been on paper since the fifteenth of December and not in anybody's head since the day before that, and that is the whole of the price, and I have paid it in four seconds in a room in front of thirty people and asked for it to be minuted, and the minute prints that I asked, and it does not print her.** **A later batch may not improve the sentence, may not attribute it to anybody else, and may not have it turn into a rule.**

9. **The seven resolutions, in order, and the sixth of the six is folded in at the back of his own book, and the seventh is written on purpose.** **The list, printed twice in Chapter 113: a tin, a coat, a yard, a table in the rain, a counter, and a book — five places and one sentence said out loud in a yard in front of about nineteen people, and the sentence is the worst of the six because it is not a place and cannot be put anywhere.** Then, in Chapter 120, a seventh, written on the twenty-first of May: ***Twenty-first of May. The item is open. I did not answer it, and I did not answer it for a reason, and the reason is not going in the letter.*** — **in his own book, not on the letter, and it is a sentence with a why in it, and a sentence with a why in it is the same shape as a seventh column, and the seventh resolution is the same as the sixth, and that is the first one he has made on purpose about a thing that was going to happen.** The resolution Lena Marr gave him on the thirtieth and the reason it is the last of its kind: **fold it in at the back of your own book and put the date on the fold in your own hand and do not take it out again, and that is the last time anybody in this district is going to be able to say a thing to you about a piece of paper and be right, because I am not going to do it again either.** The folded sheet carries **the thirtieth of April written on the fold in his own hand**, a heading in the seventh column, one line under it, and a second heading not written. **A later batch may not remove it, may not read it, may not have it found, may not have it copied, and may not make it a document.**

10. **A working day with a person in it who is not the protagonist, and the punch roll, and half an answer to the volume's third question.** **Nessa Bray, Line 3 conductor, thirty-eight, sixteen years on the run**, registered from Volume 02 Batch 0001 and unused until Chapter 112. **Out of Crown Heights at seven minutes to eight, nineteen calls out and nineteen back, and the twentieth is a siding, and it is not a call and it does not have a door on this side, and there is no form in this city for a place a car goes to be looked at.** And the object: **the roll — about nine inches of paper in a wooden case, a hole through it for every person that gets on, off a machine at the end of a run and into a tray; the only thing in this city that knows how many people were on that car and it does not know who one of them is, and that is not a defect, and she said so before he could and he would have got it wrong.** And the idea, in her mouth and half of the volume's standing third question: **the instrument a person cannot produce is the thing a person produces about nine hundred times a day, and nobody in this city has ever asked her to make it because they already have it.** And the limits, which are hers and which are not a symbol: **I am not a symbol and I am not a survivor and I am not going to be called that… and a person who has thanked somebody is a person who has asked.** **The three asks she made in December — the shed shut, the carbon to stay in the drawer, the names not to be read in a yard in the rain — were all got in about nine seconds and she has never thanked anybody and is not going to start. She was not asked about the sixteenth of December, or the thirty-one, or a printed sheet, by anybody in these ten chapters, and she said in December that she is going to be asked by nine people whether it was the best day's work this district has ever done and she is going to say no.**

11. **The changed method and the recovery, both somebody else's work, and neither of them a word.** **Corin Mabey, Line 3 driver, fifty-one, twenty-nine years, who came back with the car on the twenty-second of December and has not been able to say a sentence since the Friday and is not hurt**, in the chapter that is his, on Friday the first of May, out at about ten past seven and in at about twenty past six. **Eleven hours, eleven words, all of them to a woman of thirty-eight on a different figure of three, and none of them his.** The changed method, one sentence, and it belongs to two people who have never spoken and whom he is not going to introduce: ***the route is the doors, and the sheet is a print of the man who has the route, and a print cannot go out and come back.*** And the limits, which are hers: **she could give him the doors in the order they come, in the dark, backwards, and could not tell him why the seventh is before the eighth on a Tuesday and after it on a Friday, and has never needed to.** And the recovery, which is not a fact and not a document and cost nothing and is not trusted: **a man who cannot talk is the only person in this district I can sit next to for a whole working day without putting anybody in a column.** **A later batch may not have him speak, may not cure him, may not make the silence a device, and may not ask him about a printed sheet.**

12. **A yard man with a flag, and an hour in his book from December with nothing behind it, and nobody has asked him about it in five months.** **A man of about sixty-three on the ramp at Crown Heights, who has never looked inside a car in twenty-two years and was not asked to and is not going to, whose book says the time a car came in and nothing else and was right at 16.41 and would have been right at 16.36.** The reason, in nine words, unasked, to a man with a pen out: **if I look inside a car then I know something about a car, and what I know about a car is that it came in… there is no box anywhere in this yard for what I know and I would rather be the man with the flag.** And then: **there was a day in December that my book has an hour in that has not got anything behind it, and I have not asked about it and I am not going to, and my book is a book about times and not about cars, and the hour is in it and nothing is, and I have had five months to be asked and nobody has.** **A later batch may not ask him, may not have him look inside a car, may not put a column on his book, and must not confuse him with the man of fifty-one in a room with a machine in it or with the man of about fifty in a works jacket.**

13. **The boundary at the low end, and a boundary is a place where two people agreed to have stopped, and the plate stays in the grass.** **Saturday the second of May, a hundred and forty feet, one end a corner in a street and the other end a fence, walked on his own because one of two pegs put back in on the twenty-eighth was in the road surface and the other was about eleven inches out in a gutter and neither was wrong.** *A boundary is not a line. It is two men starting at two ends and walking towards each other, and the boundary is where they meet, and if they do not meet there is no boundary and there is a piece of ground that two departments will each believe they have.* At about eleven a works gang on the fortnightly inspection walked the frontage and **stopped at the fence, because a gang stops at a fence**, and **for about four minutes the two ends of a hundred and forty feet of boundary were at the same place, for no reason at all, and a boundary needs two ends and does not need two surveyors and does not need anybody to know what a boundary is and certainly does not need a plate.** And the finding: **a boundary is a place where two people agreed to have stopped**; a peg is a point and a boundary is not a point and a peg only says where somebody started; **there is no column for a peg in a gutter and there is no column for a peg anywhere.** And Roy Pryce, unasked: **the low end floods about twice in four years and the gutter is the only thing on that length that moves, and there is nobody out here to set it, and there has not been since the eleventh of October… a form cannot be wrong in a direction.** And the nine words that are the district's standing boundary condition: **do not go over the fence, and not because I have told you not to. There is nobody on the far side to stop you and there has not been for six years, and that is exactly why you should not go, and I have not got a better reason than that and I am not going to look for one.** **The plate has been in the grass since Monday the sixth of April; it is twenty-six days on the second of May and forty-five days on the twenty-first, it is face down, the grass has closed over the place where it went down, and that is a fact about a field and not a document, and he wrote it down for the third time and said it was the last. A later batch may not report the plate, may not put it back and write it down, may not have anybody tell the works, may not have Milo Rook go on the far side of that fence, and may not have anybody say out loud that it was one of the eleven.**

14. **The escalation, and it is a letter, and item six is four lines deep and it is the first wrong-shaped form in this volume that is wrong in the direction of a FIGURE and not a why.** **On the authority's own paper, Friday the first of May, ten past eleven, read and then put face down on his own desk: CAR 214 — LINE THREE. RETURN OF SERVICE PARTICULARS, four paragraphs.** *1.* The return for the quarter is not complete at **item 6, which is the number of persons carried.** *2.* **The roll for that working is accounted for and is not in dispute. The roll carries the count. The roll does not carry the count in figures on the working return, and the working return calls for figures.** *3.* **The district is asked to state in writing the number of persons carried on the working of 07:52 off Crown Heights on Tuesday the sixteenth day of December last. The district is the only party to which this office has been able to address correspondence in respect of that working.** *4.* **A reply is requested by the twenty-first day of May. If no reply is received the item will be returned as not stated and the quarter will close with the item standing open.** And the finding: **this city already knows the number, has had it since the sixteenth of December, and has it on a piece of paper in a wooden case in a tray, and what is missing is not a fact. What is missing is a man with a pen putting a fact that already exists onto a line that somebody will read in about four years.** And Cyril Sale's reading of it, in a doorway, unasked: **this is the first time a form has asked a district a question that a person has to be in to answer, and about eleven people in this district are in to answer every day and not one of them can be printed, and the form has got a box and the box is four lines deep and it is going to sit there open until the quarter is closed and nobody is going to be angry about it and nobody is going to be blamed for it, and in about four years the item will be a line in a book in another building and the line will be the whole of what this district is on the record as having done in December.** **The item is standing OPEN. Not a fault, not a nil, not a zero, not a defect, not a query, and not a form about a person. A later batch may not answer it, may not have anybody supply a number, may not have anybody supply a refusal, and may not close it.**

15. **THE LINE OF THE TWELFTH OF JANUARY WAS USED FOR THE FIRST TIME IN FIVE MONTHS, and it was used as a shield, and the person who said that it might be the other thing is Mara Quill in a corridor, and she is not certain and she has not softened it.** **Monday the fourth of May at about half past ten, in about nine words and then six minutes of not saying any more about it: *nobody has to answer that — and nobody has to not answer it either, and that is the new part… in January the protection was ours, we asked for it, we knew what it was for, and it cost a woman of sixty from her own name and we paid that on the fourteenth in front of thirty people. A letter about a car does not know we exist… this is the first time anybody has used it and I do not know yet whether it is the shield or the gag.*** And then: **I think it has always been both and that January was the first time it cost anything, and I have never said that to you before and I am saying it now, and I would like you to notice that I am saying it in a corridor on a Monday morning about a letter and not in a room about a paragraph, and that is the whole difference between the two and it has taken me eight years and a hearing to be able to say it.** **The line stands. It has not been removed, not been varied, and has not lapsed, and the answer of the fourth of December stands behind it, and the letter is from a tram authority and the minute is a survey department's and the letter will never see the minute. A later batch may not remove the line, may not vary its condition, may not have the condition lapse, and may not discharge the order by another route.**

16. **A refusal that is correct, and a cost that is not curable, and neither of them is paid by Lena Marr or by the woman of sixty.** **Monday the fourth of May at about half past three, at a gate in a high wall: Pell Harrow, seventeen, refuses in about nine seconds and is right. *I will not take it… the moment a courier is on a thing, the courier is the record. That is the whole of it and I have been a courier for two years… the boy cannot get out from under a piece of paper about a morning by being forty.*** And the refusal of the record itself: **I would say it, and then it would be true, and it would be the first true thing anybody has said about that morning since the Friday in December and it would be about me.** And the third of the three things in the timetable division's standing rule, named by him and not by the surveyor: **do not send the man from the round… somebody wrote that rule for a reason and the reason is a boy with a satchel, and I am not going to be the reason a rule holds in a different direction in the year I am nineteen.** **THE COST, AND IT IS NOT CURABLE: the round is one man short, it has been one man short since the twenty-eighth of December, and there are two reasons and they are NOT joined — the twenty-two days up the south, unasked and going to stay unasked, which is a refusal of a man of forty-one made in about four seconds in January and held four months; and the fact that a round is carried by a postman and a postman is on a form and the form wants a depot and a depot is an address, and a district with no rateable unit and no address cannot have a depot, and has not had one since the eleventh of October six years ago when a street name came off an index. So a boy of seventeen does seven stops in eleven streets on a Monday and a Thursday and takes a handcart through a gate in a high wall, and there is nobody to send with him, and there will be nobody in June and none in the fourth year, and it cannot be cured from inside this district, and it is not an injustice and nobody has done anything wrong, and it is a design.** A later batch may not cure it, may not join the two reasons, and may not have anybody ask Pell Harrow about the twenty-two days.

17. **The fourth and last comparison of the four return-route copies, on Monday the FOURTH of May, and the number has not changed and the fact behind it has.** **Four copies out of four places, version and date compared, nothing else read, in the half hour after four when the counter is shut. Three of the four say version one and one says version two and the fourth has been read and will not be described. He was not in the room and the four lines of the reason are: a comparison of four people is not his, and the holder is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be, and the four are four people, and he is one of the four. On the outside of the envelope, in pencil, in her own hand: *1 of 4. — 1.iv.* — the same number since the nineteenth of January, meaning a different piece of paper every month, and THE FACT THAT CHANGED THIS MONTH IS THAT SHE DID NOT REWRITE IT: she has changed it every month since the nineteenth of January and on the fourth of May she did not, and the corner of that envelope is four weeks old and says the first of April on a piece of paper that was last handled on Monday. She is not going to say why and is not going to write anything next to it, and she has said *I wanted to write something else next to it* three times and is not going to say it a fourth. And the second half of her own rule, said out loud for the first time: the second half is the whole of it, and the second half is the only part of it that is about this envelope, and he has taken the first half four times and left the second half four times. A later batch may not resolve the disagreement, may not change the number, may not have her rewrite the date, may not have her find the fourth, and may not stage a comparison before the first working day of any month.**

18. **A list is read by the people on it and by the person who made it, and a minute is read by nobody until somebody produces it in about four years, and the two are in the same building about forty feet apart and do not know about each other.** **Tuesday the fifth of May, about ten past four, in a corridor, about four hundred people walk past a board every working day and one of them stood about forty feet from a door and was spoken to by nobody for about twenty minutes, for the second time in three weeks.** The six items of the printed business of the day are quoted verbatim and **the district's matter is not on it and could not have been**, and **the fifth of May did nothing to this district in about six hours, correctly, and it did it on a printed side served by hand to a door.** The finding: **a list is read by the people who are on it and by the person who made it, and that is the entire readership of a list, and it is about eleven people and four hundred passers-by who do not stop; a minute is read by nobody — not by the parties, not by the department, not by the chair — until whoever produces it, in about four years, on behalf of somebody who is not in the room. Those are two different readerships of two different objects about forty feet apart in the same building and they do not know about each other and they do not have to, and a district does not get from one to the other, it is in one or the other, and this district is in neither.** And: **the room is not the list and the list is not the room, and about four hundred people a day walk past the difference and nobody has ever made a form of it, and that is not an oversight, that is the design.** **A later batch may not go and sit in that room, may not put the matter on any list, may not add or remove an item from the business of the fifth of May, and may not make a form out of a matter's absence from a list.**

19. **Two instruments, one district, and no line drawn between them — and a head and a number are the same sentence and he has not joined them.** **Wednesday the sixth of May, about half past four in a yard, and he counted the fortnight out loud and got it wrong by one day and corrected it and about four people heard him do it and nobody said anything.** The two instruments: **a book with a night in it, which is mine, and a sheet with a day in it, which is the district's, and one has eleven entries so far this month and the other has five correct blanks, and both are about this district and neither can see the other, and I noticed that on Wednesday morning and I have written down that I noticed, and noticing is not joining, and I have joined them four times in my head today and written it down once.** And: **a book with a night in it is one night closer to a record and a sheet with a day in it is the only instrument in this district that has never needed a reason, and I have got both of them and I am not going to put them next to each other and I do not know why and I have not written down why.** And the sentence that cannot be joined: **a head is the only repository in this city that cannot be produced against anybody** (Lena Marr, a doorway, the thirtieth of April) **and a number is the one thing about four people I am allowed to have** (Monday, a counter) — **and they are the same sentence, said by two people in eleven streets who have never met and have no reason to and are not going to, and the moment two of them are the same sentence there is a third one, and a third one is a pattern, and a pattern is me deciding that two things are the same, and that is the reason and I do not like the reason, and I have now put the reason down once and I am not going to put it down again on purpose. And I want it written down, in my own hand, tonight, that I did not join them on paper.** And the cost of the not-joining, which is new: **that is the first time in seven months I have had the whole of a thing in my hands and not put it down, and I did not put it down on purpose and I was not brave about it and nobody helped me, and a man of fifty-eight with a spade helped me and he does not know he helped me.** **A later batch may not join the head to the number, may not join the punch roll to either, and may not have any of the three explained by a person.**

20. **The fact behind a number, and it is the block's smallest and best idea: a version is a fact about a person, and she wrote nothing on the first of April, so the number on the envelope is a record of a person deciding not to write something.** *The number on the outside of the envelope says a version and a date. A version is a fact about a person, because only people make versions. The date is the first of April. She has not written anything on the first of April. So the number on the outside of that envelope is a record of a person deciding not to write something, and it is the only mark in that arrangement which is about a person and cannot be produced against one, because the thing it is about has not been written.* **Worked out in about nine seconds at a counter on the sixth of May, put in his own book, and not put in a yard, and she did not tell him and he did not ask, and he has written down for the fifth time that she was not asked and the fifth is the one he stops at.** The ninth question is the only one he actually wants and he is not going to ask it.

21. **The condition column is the only empty column in that office that nobody has ever queried, and the reason is that it is the only one of the six that is about what a length was like, and a man of forty-six said it once and said he was not going to say it again.** **Friday the eighth of May, about eleven o'clock, in a corridor, four rooms above a fitter's shop, and it is the sixth true sentence anybody in this district has said to him in seven months and he does not know it was the sixth.** *That column is the only one in this building that is allowed to be empty… the other five columns are about what we did. That one is about what it was like. And nobody in this city has ever asked a gang what a length was like, and the day one of them does there is going to be a seventh column on that sheet with a heading in it and a man's name against it, and it will be my name or Roy's.* And then the second half, which is the standing condition of the nil on line five: **nobody has ever asked about line five in nine weeks and a thing that has never been asked about for nine weeks is not protected, it is just not yet asked about, and the only reason it has not been asked about is that a man in another building has been asking about a box on a different form, and that is all that is holding it, and it is not holding it, it is just in the way.** And the prediction, printed on the seventh and wrong on the best and right on the not-a-number: **you are going to write something on Monday the nineteenth, and it is going to be the best thing you have ever written, and it is not going to be a number of persons and it is not going to be a why, and I am going to read it in about four years and I am not going to be able to tell whether you refused or ran out of answers.** **The line-five figure is nine weeks on the seventh and eighth of May and ELEVEN weeks on the twenty-first, and a later batch may not query line five, may not answer the two queries, may not have the foreman read the sixth line, and may not have anybody discover the sixth line.**

22. **The orientation ledger, the Stage 2 cost, and the Stage 2 gain, and none of them moved, and the written line home did not move either.** **The remembered-route list stands at TEN**, the original eight intact, none removed, all ten marked by somebody who is not him, **and nothing was added and nothing was lost in Chapters 111–120, and the reason it is ten and not eleven is printed in Chapter 111 and in Chapter 120: he did not cross, and that is the first time in his working life he has been glad of a number and he does not trust being glad of a number. The ninth crossing's failure to cost one is still unexplained and is not explained by anything in this block.** **Milo Rook crossed nothing.** Iona Sen's tally is unchanged at **nine lines**, the paper is Hettie Brann's and the pocket is Dr Sen's, **and he has not looked at it since the thirteenth of January and he wrote on the twentieth of May that he is not going to look at it in June.** **There is no stabilisation in Chapters 111–120: he held nothing on a car for eleven hours, he held nothing at a fence, he held nothing at a gate, and he went into nowhere.** **The written line home went on the twenty-fourth of November, which is day one, and the figures in this block's Field Book entries are 155, 156, 158, 159, 160, 161, 162, 163, 165 and 179**, on the twenty-seventh and twenty-eighth of April, the thirtieth of April, the first, second, third, fourth, fifth and seventh of May and the twenty-first of May, **and two figures are carried arithmetic with no count written in the chapter that owns the day — 157 on the twenty-ninth of April and 164 on the sixth of May — and a later batch may carry them and may not quote them as a chapter's words.** **The full inherited register carried forward unchanged and not re-derived: 92, 103, 108, 112, 115, 119, 123, 127, 128, 129, 136, 138, 142, 144, 148, 149, 150, 151, 153**, and before those **35, 39, 43, 45, 46, 50, 51, 52, 54, 57, 59** and **67, 68, 69, 70, 71, 71, 74, 81, 88, 89.** The line is blank throughout, **with four marks in FOUR hands and none of them his, the fourth written on the first of April at a counter in about four seconds by a woman who was not asked**, and **on the eighteenth of May he deliberately stopped writing down the sentence that says it is there, having written it in seven of the previous eight entries, and nobody was told and nobody was asked to fill it in and the district's not-asking remains a refusal and not a kindness.** The Lark token was cold throughout, was not taken out, and is **the hundred and forty-first to the hundred and sixty-fifth day** on the dates in the register, **which is 141 on the twenty-seventh of April, 142 on the twenty-eighth, 144 on the thirtieth, 145 on the first of May, 146 on the second, 147 on the third, 148 on the fourth, 149 on the fifth, 151 on the seventh and 165 on the twenty-first, with 143 on the twenty-ninth of April and 150 on the sixth of May carried arithmetic and not written.**

23. **The Field Book's pairs, and the figure did not move, and the reason is new and it is in the method's own logic.** **TWENTY-SEVEN: two sixes, SEVEN threes, one nine, FOUR twos, three fives, one eight, one twenty-five, two sevens, and SIX fours** — four of the fours in March and two in April, five of the six since the eleventh of March. **He has an entry in the book on every one of the twenty-six nights from Sunday the twenty-sixth of April to Thursday the twenty-first of May, so that every interval since the twenty-sixth of April is a single day, and a single day is not a pair whether or not either of the two days has anything on it, and the list has not moved in twenty-six days.** **THE FIGURE MUST NOT BE READ AS TWENTY-EIGHT, TWENTY-NINE OR THIRTY, AND NO NEW INTERVAL OF TWO DAYS OR MORE WAS WRITTEN IN THIS BLOCK, AND A LATER BATCH MAY NOT WRITE ONE WITHOUT ADDING IT AND RECOUNTING.** The four threes that were here in December are the four that were here in December and are not collapsed; the fifth was added in the first week of February, the sixth on the twenty-third of February and the seventh on the eighteenth of March, and none of the original four is one of the other three. There is one eight. There is no thirty-six, no eleventh pair, no thirteenth pair, no twenty-first pair and no twenty-second pair. The threes are seven, the sixes two, the twos four, the fives three, the sevens two and the fours six. **And the new sentence, printed in Chapter 120 and it is the first time he has wanted a number to go up and could not make it: an interval of one day is not a pair whether or not either of the two days has anything on it, and I have proved that twenty-six times since the twenty-sixth of April, and the number has not moved, and that is the first thing in seven months that I have wanted to make go up and could not.** And the standing diagnosis, unchanged: he joins none of them to any other and has written that he does not know what twenty-seven of a thing means, and a man of about sixty has been proving the same sentence on a corner of a wall for nineteen years and does not know he has been written down five times.

24. **The relationship, milestone 2, and the two things in it that are new are both wrong and he has written down that he knows they are wrong.** **Nothing romantic has happened and nothing is going to. No kiss, no declaration, no jealousy scene, no rescue. The Lark Street grievance is not named, not resolved, not softened and not referred to in any of these ten chapters.** **Mara Quill appears twice, both work: a corridor at about half past ten on the fourth of May, and, in the same corridor and the same conversation, the not-telling.** **Her custody register page is untouched for the fifth consecutive block: it is in a drawer with a coat in it, and it was not produced, not named, not confessed to, and not mentioned in dialogue in any of the ten chapters, and he did not ask, and he is forbidden to ask.** **What is new and is not warm, and it is the closest the two of them have come in six months and it is about paperwork: he told her he had not told her about the season, for the third week, and she said *I am not going to ask you about it, and I have a page in a drawer with a coat in it and I have not produced it for four consecutive months and you have not asked, and I have noticed the count from my side and I have not mentioned it, and we are two people keeping a number about each other and the number is the same and neither of us has said it out loud, and that is the most intimate thing that has happened between us and it is a document neither of us has mentioned. Do not improve on that in a yard.*** **And he has not asked her about the seventh of January: that is seven times now, and he has stopped counting and started dating, which is worse, because a date is a column.** Her second letter remains unsent and is not mentioned in these ten chapters. **A later batch may not have her hand the custody register page over as a favour, may not have her confess unless the scene earns it at a cost, and he must not ask.**

25. **No new grammar, faction, antagonist, final enemy, or fourth kind of place.** **The grammars in use in these ten chapters are Keepsake (the Row and its Wednesday) and the block names no new dominant grammar. A length is still a third kind of place and is not a remnant, not a failure state, and not a form of any of the seven grammars. The Row is still a Keepsake remnant. A settlement plate is a plate, a fence is a fence, a peg is a point and not a boundary, a punch roll is a piece of paper about nine inches long, a yard book is a book about times, a letter is a letter, an envelope is an envelope, a gang sheet is a gang sheet and a working timetable is a working timetable, and none of them is a kind of place and none of them has been described as one.** **A boundary is a place where two people agreed to have stopped, and that is a statement about a boundary and not a fourth kind of place, and a later batch may not convert it into one.** **Sel Iri does not appear. Halden Vey does not appear and is not named. Orrin Sable does not appear. Tarin Kest does not appear. Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–120. The man in the works jacket is not seen once in these ten chapters, nobody was asked to look, and no fifth sighting and no fifth refusal was made by anybody.** **The three clues from Volume 01 appear in no chapter of this block as a document and are joined to nothing, and no fifth refusal to join them was made by anybody. Two documents still carry a pair of marks and not three; the twenty-first's Registry certification is drawn where the two confirmed marks are pressed; Kest has not matched the strings; and a fold is still not a chronology — no document's age in this block is established by a mark on it, and the twenty-one days he spent reading a routing slip last winter bought him a method and not a date.** **The three things under the brick are still three and the run number has not moved.** **The office of the First Surveyor is named nowhere in this block and there is still no person behind it, and the block does not make it request, receive or act on anything.** The one thing built for one purpose and found the wrong shape for another, and it is new: **item six of a return of service particulars, which is four lines deep and was built in about 1961 for a conductor to fill in off a punch roll, and it wants a figure, and the roll already has the figure and the roll is not a document anybody can produce, and the district cannot put one on the line and cannot leave it off, and the quarter is going to close with the item standing open.** Others: the condition column, which is about what a length was like and about which nobody has ever asked a gang; a peg, which is a point; a print of a man, which cannot go out and come back; a boundary walked from one end.

26. **Pell Harrow, and Tern, and the standing refusal, held and untested.** **He is on the round, out at about ten past eight and in at about four, and he goes through the quay section gate on Mondays and Thursdays because the district has no address and it is the only gate a cart can get out of on a working day.** **In Chapter 117 he refuses correctly and in about nine seconds, he names the reason himself, and he is right, and a later batch may not have him carry anything, may not send him anywhere, and may not make him ask anybody anything.** **Tern is still not found, the list in the tin is not a finding, Ostrow confirms nothing, and no chapter in this block rescued anybody or asked anything about the twenty-two days up the south, and no batch may make anybody ask him in order to report them.** **His card under the brick is blank and has been since the nineteenth of January, it is one of the three things under that brick, and nobody has taken it and nobody is going to.** The standing rule about that brick — *a card under a brick is a thing and not a job, and about four times a day somebody looks at a job and decides it is not theirs* — **was acted on in Chapter 117 and not by him and not by Milo Rook, and the person who acted on it was a man of forty-one on no list at all, and it is the fifth time he has said it out loud.** **The timetable division's standing refusal held and was not tested: ask nobody, do not come here, do not send the man from the round, and no working person was sent anywhere, and the reason it held is printed in Chapter 118 and it is new: a rule holds by not being tested. Barrow is not in these ten chapters, has not been asked anything, has not given his name, and the run number has not moved.** He has no power, his mark still holds for a working shift and not overnight, and it was not used and is not going to be.

27. **Volume 01's people carry Volume 01's prices into this block and not one of them is refunded, and this batch adds ZERO references and says so.** **Ada Fenn is not named in Chapters 111–120 and her name is not written once in them, and THE PHRASE *a woman of sixty-eight on nothing* IS NOT USED IN ANY OF THE TEN CHAPTERS — not in the yard, not in a Field Book entry, and not in the carried list, where the absence is stated as a count and the phrase itself is not written. The addition is ZERO and the count stands at eight across eight chapters of Volume 02 and is not nine.** The woman on the fourth floor of Cedar Court is not mentioned in these ten chapters at all, so that the two sixty-eights cannot be folded, and the woman of sixty on the second landing is a different and unnamed resident and is the ninth of the twenty-six names and is not named either. Old Mabb is asleep and is not named and is not woken. Ivy Marsden is not named, does not appear and is not told anything; the page with her name struck through it is not read, not removed and not mentioned, **and Milo Rook did not go near the north end of Saltmarket Row in this block and has not gone since the nineteenth of February.** A hundred and forty feet of named frontage stands behind a fence with a settlement plate on it, **and the plate is in the grass and has been since the sixth of April and is forty-five days old on the twenty-first of May and nobody has reported it and nobody has told the works and nobody has said out loud that it was one of the eleven.** A conductor of thirty in a printed rule book whose own hearing still has no date. A woman of fifty-four with a plot number and no bill in six years. Two children lost in October and still lost. A mechanic of sixty-one on a drainage record forever who will not swear. **Two and four a head a week, in advance, week by week, with no term, for two hundred and fifty-two people in a building with no number, and a woman of about thirty-four who drafted the prohibition that is stopping a woman of sixty from her own name, and who said yes in about four seconds in a room on the fourteenth of April and asked for it to be minuted, and the minute prints that she asked** — and none of it was paid again in these ten chapters. **Owen Fitch's name is still on five copies of a sheet of paper with a floor that is not his.** **The two money totals are carried forward unchanged, neither is printed in these ten chapters, and the multiplication is still never shown. Roy Pryce's reason in the hours column is still called nine words and is not re-counted anywhere in this block.**

28. **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, eight words, on the inside of a gate in chalk, and it is not given to anybody.** **At about half past four on Wednesday the twentieth of May, on the inside of a gate in a yard, in chalk, in his own hand: *Whoever holds the pen is not the writer.*** Nobody photographed it, nobody copied it, no date was written against it, and **he has not told a woman of sixty-one, who has said twice in six months that the moment a thing can be used it is in use and whom he has believed both times, and he has not told a works clerk of forty-six, and there are now three people in this city who know there is writing on that gate and he does not yet know for how long he is going to keep it that way.** **The batch's five prior residues are inherited unchanged and were not restated: *A trade outlives its pitch* (Ch 66), *A length is missed before it is lost* (Ch 77), *Weather uses whatever door is open* (Ch 89), *A habit is not a calendar* (Ch 95), *It is dated before it is read* (Ch 108).** A later batch may not use residue more than once in ten chapters and may not repeat any of the six sentences.

29. **The pressures, and the shape of the batch, stated for a later writer.** The batch is **the second half of the volume's last movement: what a district does in the twenty-six days after a hearing that gave it nothing and a season that ended, when the machinery is still printing and the chair is not.** It contains **one working day with a person in it** (Ch 112, Nessa Bray, and Ch 114, Corin Mabey, and Ch 119's yard), **one field or boundary scene** (Ch 115), **one failure** (Ch 113), **one changed method and one recovery by the same person and neither of them a word** (Ch 114), **one correct refusal** (Ch 117, Pell Harrow), **one cost that is not curable and is not paid by Lena Marr or by the woman of sixty** (Ch 117, the round one man short), **an escalation that is a document and is a letter** (Ch 116), **the answer in working people's mouths in two new mouths** (Ch 114's flag-man, Ch 119's works clerk), and **the standing question** (Ch 120). **Six moments in which a thing somebody built for one purpose turned out to be the wrong shape for another: item six of a return of service particulars, four lines deep, built in about 1961 for a conductor to fill in off a punch roll, and it wants a figure (Ch 116); a working sheet, which is a print of the man who has the route and a print cannot go out and come back (Ch 114); a peg, which is a point, and a boundary is not a point (Ch 115); a settlement plate, which is what a decision gets put on from the far side and the decision is made in the middle by two people walking towards each other and neither of them is at the fence (Ch 115); a yard book, which is a book about times and not about cars and has an hour in it from December with nothing behind it (Ch 114); and a second side of a sheet, which is a day and is right about five days it cannot see (Ch 111, Ch 119).** **The block's boundary is honest: the order is not discharged, the name is not on the street name index, the adjournment is not moved and the matter is not re-adjourned, the removed sentence is not restored and the reference is not answered, the fifth sighting and the fifth refusal are both not made, a crossing is not required and not made, nobody goes to Mireward, there is no fourth nil, the letter is not answered and the item stands open, the run number does not move, no item is added to or removed from the printed business of the fifth of May, the plate is not reported, the condition column stays empty, the three things under the brick are three, the volume's final image is not written and was not approached, and the block closes on: the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything, and that is a list of about fourteen other people and not one of them has been asked to be on it, and in twenty-six nights in a row I have not found a way to do anything else.**

## What Chapters 121–130 fixed on the page, in the same form as CR-013

Each item below is now prose rather than plan, and each is load-bearing in a way a later batch could break without intending to. **None of them changes a premise, a world rule, a power rule, the antagonist ladder, or the planned ending. Every figure here was made from the manuscript and is quotable from a chapter in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0121.md` … `chapter-0130.md`, after the writer's own weekday, day-count, pair-count, walk-and-rack, word-count and meta-language pass.** **THE FINAL IMAGE IS WRITTEN ONCE, IN CHAPTER 130 ONLY, AND IT IS PAIRED WITH AN EXPLICIT NEGATIVE: on the fifteenth of June he stood in that office for about nine minutes and asked the clerk NOTHING, on the sixteenth he stood in it for about twenty minutes and asked her nothing at all, and the chapter says she has not been asked about a morning in December by anybody including him, and every one of those sentences is now true of the scene because the two questions the chapter originally put in his mouth were taken out of it and the clerk volunteered both answers instead. A later volume may not put a question into that room, may not have the clerk volunteer twice, and may not have anybody ask a timetable clerk anything.** Chapters 121–130 are the **seventh and last block of Volume 02, the volume close**, and they inherit the Chapters 111–120, 101–110, 91–100, 81–90, 71–80, 61–70 and Volume 01 lists above whole. **Volume 02 is CLOSED at Chapter 130.**

1. **THE VOLUME'S FINAL IMAGE, in full, in the words of Chapter 130, and the volume closes there. The recovered timetable contains a destination that does not exist, and the passengers' names are real, and both of them are in the same book on the same page, and they got there the same way, which is that a column takes what it is given.** The object is **a bound working timetable on a shelf four feet from a clerk's desk in a depot office at Alder Quay**, with eight columns — *day, time, working, cars, run no, crew, terminates, remarks* — **printed from a diagram that is printed from a list that is a hundred and forty years old, and a wide ruled margin down the left-hand side with a heading printed at the top of it, and the heading is *actual*, and *actual* is where a clerk writes what happened against what was booked, and it is the right shape for a time and not for anything else.** In the row for **Tuesday the sixteenth of December, 07:52 off Crown Heights, one car**, the run column and the crew column carry **the same four characters in type**, which is a crew and not a car, and the **terminates** column says **HOLLOWGATE**, which is not on the diagram, and there is no Hollowgate and there has never been one. Down about eleven inches of the ruled margin, in ink, in a small upright hand, **thirty-one names, in the order the people got on**; and the carbon for that working is face up in a tray on the same desk with **thirty-one names in a conductor's hand in the same order**, and the order is the order a hole went through nine inches of paper for every person that got on. **A destination that does not exist is a fact about a place and not about a person. There is nobody in it. Nobody came back. The printed part is the false part and the handwritten part is the true part, and they are in the same book on the same page, and there is nobody coming to either of them, and that is the only thing he has got after seven months and it is not a rescue and it is not a comfort and it is not a haunting — it is a column that took what it was given.** **The volume closes on this image. Volume 03 opens on a different question — *why does weather memory threaten a region the Registry claims is stable?* — and nothing in Chapters 121–130 touches it, and no Volume 03 outline, brief, prompt or chapter has been written, and the writer may not write one.** A later volume may not make the names into a claim, a curse, a debt, an inherited role, a surviving presence or a person; may not say that anybody in that timetable came back; may not give Hollowgate a destination, a location, a meaning, a person or a knock; may not have anybody ask a question of it; and may not have anybody — including Milo Rook — be one of the four, or the five.

2. **The batch's shape and its boundary.** Ten chapters, **Friday the twenty-second of May to Tuesday the sixteenth of June, twenty-six days**, and it includes **Monday the first of June, Tuesday the second, Wednesday the third, Thursday the fourth, Friday the fifth, Saturday the sixth, Monday the eighth, Tuesday the ninth, Wednesday the tenth, Thursday the eleventh, Friday the twelfth, Monday the fifteenth and Tuesday the sixteenth.** It contains **one working day with a person in it** (Ch 122, Dr Iona Sen; and Ch 128, an ordinary Friday in order), **one field or boundary scene** (Ch 125), **one failure** (Ch 123), **one changed method and one recovery, both somebody else's work** (Ch 124), **two correct refusals** (Ch 127's counter and Ch 129's nine minutes in which he asked Hester Coombe nothing at all — **EIGHT questions, two of which were in his mouth for about a second and a half and six of which were never in it, and she answered two of the eight unasked and he has written down that he has no idea what she decided about that and is not going to find out; the state layer no longer says he asked nothing when the chapter had him ask two, which is what CR-043 records**), **a cost that is not curable and paid by neither Lena Marr nor the woman of sixty** (Ch 127), **an escalation that is a document** (Ch 126, the return for the quarter ending the thirtieth of June arriving inside the returned envelope), **four moments in which a thing somebody built for one purpose turned out to be the wrong shape for another, and the running count in the manuscript now ascends and every figure is printed where it is claimed: the SEVENTH in Ch 123, the EIGHTH in Ch 124, the NINTH in Ch 125 and the TENTH in Ch 126, and Chapters 124 and 125 now carry their figures instead of leaving the count to jump**, and **one Atlas residue in ten chapters, nine words, in Chapter 130, given to nobody.**

3. **THE WEEKDAY CHAIN, extended and not re-derived, and the round's two days.** From Batch 0006, which left the chain at **21 May Thursday**: **22nd Fri, 23rd Sat, 24th Sun, 25th Mon, 26th Tue, 27th Wed, 28th Thu, 29th Fri, 30th Sat, 31st Sun, 1st June Mon, 2nd Tue, 3rd Wed, 4th Thu, 5th Fri, 6th Sat, 7th Sun, 8th Mon, 9th Tue, 10th Wed, 11th Thu, 12th Fri, 13th Sat, 14th Sun, 15th Mon, 16th Tue**, and then **17th Wed, 18th Thu, 19th Fri, 20th Sat, 21st Sun, 22nd Mon, 23rd Tue, 24th Wed, 25th Thu, 26th Fri, 27th Sat, 28th Sun, 29th Mon, 30th Tue.** **THE THIRD OF MAY WAS A SUNDAY, as Batch 0006 printed, and nothing in this batch has re-derived it. THE COURIER ROUND GOES ON MONDAYS AND THURSDAYS ONLY AND HAS SINCE THE SECOND OF JANUARY, and it goes out at about ten past eight and comes in at about four, and nothing in Chapters 121–130 puts it on a Wednesday, a Friday, a Saturday, a Sunday or a Tuesday: it runs on the eighth and the eleventh of June, it is absent on the first of June and the fifteenth, and the Friday the twelfth of June says so on the page.** **And Tuesday the sixteenth of June is the same weekday as Tuesday the sixteenth of December, six months earlier, and that is a fact about a calendar and the only thing about the two dates that is the same, and it is printed once and is not a resonance and is not to be built on.** The chapter dates, all on the page: 22 May and 1 June (Ch 121, Fri and Mon), 2 June (Ch 122, Tue), 3–4 June (Ch 123, Wed–Thu), 5 June (Ch 124, Fri), 6 June (Ch 125, Sat), 8–9 June (Ch 126, Mon–Tue), 10–11 June (Ch 127, Wed–Thu), 12 June (Ch 128, Fri), 15 June (Ch 129, Mon), 16 June (Ch 130, Tue).

4. **The gang's week, the walks, the racks and the second tin's arithmetic.** The gang is on the back length **Mondays and Thursdays at 07.30**, the weekly sheet covers Monday to Sunday, it goes into the rack on the **Friday**, the duplicate into a tin, and the duplicate into the second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room of the Civic Atlas Office under a repair schedule. **THE WALKS IN THIS BLOCK ARE THE TWENTY-FIFTH AND TWENTY-EIGHTH OF MAY AND THE FIRST, FOURTH, EIGHTH, ELEVENTH AND FIFTEENTH OF JUNE — SEVEN, and the last of them is the final walk of Volume 02, and it is announced in advance in Chapter 128 (*on Monday the fifteenth of June the gang walks*) because the fifteenth of June is a Monday and Mondays and Thursdays are the two days the gang walks. Chapter 129 is the only chapter set on the fifteenth and it contains no gang, and it does not need to, and the figure of seven is a count of days in a fixed chain and not a figure printed in a chapter. THE RACKS ARE THE TWENTY-SECOND AND TWENTY-NINTH OF MAY AND THE FIFTH AND TWELFTH OF JUNE — FOUR FRIDAYS, and four, which is the number of Fridays in the period. THE CONDITION COLUMN IS EMPTY ON EVERY ONE OF THE SEVEN WALKS AND THE REASON COLUMN IS EMPTY ON ALL SEVEN AND BOTH OF THOSE ARE CORRECT, and there is no column on that sheet that wants to know why a column is empty.** **NO FIGURE FOR THE TOTAL RUNNING COUNT OF WALKS SINCE THE FIRST OF APRIL IS PRINTED IN ANY OF THE TEN CHAPTERS, and none is printed here, because CR-023 requires every figure in this file to be quotable from a chapter that can be named and two earlier passes printed a per-month breakdown and a total that no chapter supports. THE ONLY TWO FIGURES THE MANUSCRIPT PRINTS ARE THE EIGHTEENTH, in Chapter 123, for the walk of Thursday the fourth of June, and THE TWENTIETH, in Chapter 128, which is now stated on the page as the walk of Thursday the eleventh of June. Chapter 128 also says the gang does not walk on a Friday, because a Friday is the day the sheet goes into a rack, and the two figures are therefore six days apart and a fortnight is four Mondays and Thursdays. A later volume may not print a total running count that no chapter prints.** **The second tin held TWENTY-TWO on Friday the fifteenth of May (inherited, printed in Chapter 120), TWENTY-THREE on Friday the twenty-second (Ch 121), TWENTY-FOUR on Friday the twenty-ninth (Ch 123, carried, for the Friday before the chapter), TWENTY-FIVE on Friday the fifth (Ch 124) and TWENTY-SIX on Friday the twelfth (Ch 128).** A later volume may not reduce any of these figures, may not put a word in the condition column about why, and may not turn the empty walks into a record of anything.

5. **The second side has TEN blank Wednesdays in it and the sheet is right about every one of them, and the count of the four unshowable things in five months is FOUR and there is no fifth.** The inherited seven are the eighth, fifteenth, twenty-second and twenty-ninth of April and the sixth, thirteenth and twentieth of May. **The eighth is Wednesday the twenty-seventh of May (named in Chapter 121), the ninth is Wednesday the third of June (Chapter 121, forward), the tenth is Wednesday the tenth of June (Chapter 127).** A blank on that side is a day when nobody stood in the weather, and **on every one of the ten the only person who stood in the weather in this district was a man of about sixty at a shed at Cardew Docks**, and he does not know a piece of paper exists and is not going to be told. **NOBODY IN CHAPTERS 121–130 STANDS IN THE WEATHER ON A WEDNESDAY, and the fifteen hundred yards of grass on the far side of a fence at the low end is being cut in June on a Wednesday by somebody who is not the works and not the council and is not anybody this district has a name for.** The four unshowable things in five months are a rectangle of standing water on the twenty-ninth of January, a hundred and ten yards of frontage in the rain on the nineteenth of February, a plate in a field on the sixth of April, and about nine people in eleven streets standing in the weather for nothing at all on the fifteenth of April. **The fifteenth of April is the fourth of the four and is not a series and is not a fifth thing, and nothing in Chapters 121–130 turns any of the four into a record or puts a why next to any of them or has anybody ask what this district did in April.**

6. **The plate, and the plate is the last thing he gave up in six months, and it is a decision and not a refusal.** Face down in the grass on the far side of a fence at the low end since **Monday the sixth of April**. **The plate went down on Monday the sixth of April, so every figure is the number of days from that Monday. The figures printed in the chapters are FIFTY-SIX DAYS on Monday the first of June (Ch 121), FIFTY-EIGHT on Wednesday the third (Ch 123 in the narration, and Ch 124 in the narration), FIFTY-NINE on Thursday the fourth (Ch 123, in the Field Book entry for the fourth, which is a different day and therefore a different figure), SIXTY-ONE on Saturday the sixth (Ch 125), SIXTY-SEVEN on Friday the twelfth (Ch 128) and SEVENTY-ONE on Tuesday the sixteenth (Ch 130). FIFTY-EIGHT AND FIFTY-NINE ARE BOTH RIGHT AND BOTH PRINTED, in different chapters and on different days, and no reader is to reconcile them.** The grass has closed over the place where it went down and the ground under it has gone from soft to hard and it is not going to be lifted. **On Friday the twelfth of June, at about half past five, in a doorway, a woman of sixty-three with a hand barrow asked him to tell the works that there is a plate off a fence, and said she was not asking him what it was and had not asked and was not going to, and he did not answer and did not tell the works.** **THE DATE OF THE DECISION IS THE NIGHT OF MONDAY THE FIRST OF JUNE AND IT IS THE FIRST OF JUNE IN FOUR CHAPTERS, and it was the thirteenth in this file and it is now corrected to the manuscript rather than the other way round. Ch 121: *I have moved it out of a refusal and into a decision and I have written that down tonight and told nobody.* Ch 123 and Ch 125: *I wrote that down on the first of June.* Ch 128 now says the decision was a decision before Nell Crale opened her mouth in the doorway on the twelfth and a decision after, and that the asking did not make it into one, and its Field Book says the deciding of it was done in his own hand on the first of June and was in a book in a coat before she said anything.
